> Escalation Request
> Given the overwhelming evidence of misrepresentation, coercion, and retaliation, I
> respectfully request this case be escalated to Navy Federal’s legal/compliance team for
> final review under FCBA §1666 and Visa Core Rules.
> All documentation previously submitted exceeds the evidentiary burden required under
> Visa Rule 11.3.4 and 12.5.3.2.
> Providing portal access after extracting funds under false pretenses does not cure
> misrepresentation, premature payment, or retaliation. Under Visa Rule 12.5.3.2,
> “services not provided” includes cases where services were materially misrepresented
> or later revoked.
> Limited access cannot retroactively justify a $20,600 charge, and cannot justify
> merchant "delivery" overriding rules, laws, and rights.
> Critical Evidence
> Time-stamped records show payment was processed before the contract or refund
> terms were ever disclosed.
> This violates:
> ● FCBA §1666(a)(3)(B) - billing errors include charges for services “not accepted
> or not delivered as agreed.” A payment taken prior to disclosure cannot
> constitute acceptance.
> ● Visa Core Rule 5.4.2.5 - refund policies must be disclosed before payment.
> ● Visa Core Rule 11.3.4 - merchants cannot waive or suppress chargeback rights.
> ● Restatement (Second) of Contracts §17 - requires mutual assent before
> consideration.
> ● E-SIGN Act §7001(c)(1)(A) - consent must follow disclosure of terms.
> Accordingly, any agreement signed after payment cannot legally be used as a
> defense to deny this chargeback.
> Compliance Obligations
> Any denial at this stage would constitute:
> 12323150.1.3-14
> ● Willful noncompliance with FCBA §1666(a)(3)(B),
> ● Reckless disregard of Visa Core Rules 5.4.2.5, 11.3.4, and 12.5.3.2, and
> ● Negligent handling of a billing error claim.
> This case is already part of CFPB Case #240426-14545424, Case #250825-23483188,
> and FTC Report #190055885.
> Continued denial despite documented statutory violations would expose Navy Federal -
> not just the merchant - to regulatory scrutiny, including CFPB supervisory review of
> dispute-handling practices.
> Since Navy Federal is federally chartered, dispute handling must also comply with
> NCUA/OCC supervisory standards, which require fair, good-faith review of billing error
> claims and prohibit reliance on merchant policies that conflict with federal consumer law.
> Finally, under Visa’s own Compliance Program, issuers who fail to enforce Core Rules
> risk Visa enforcement action.
> If necessary, this case can be escalated through Visa Compliance for direct intervention.
> Request
> I respectfully request immediate escalation to Navy Federal’s legal/compliance team
> and Visa Compliance for correction.
> Please confirm escalation and final review immediately.
> I trust Navy Federal will honor its consumer-first protections and cardholder advocacy
> standards by ensuring this case is resolved through a full chargeback and refund.
> While Navy issued a prior determination, that decision failed to consider critical,
> time-stamped evidence that renders the merchant’s contract void (payment before
> disclosure) and documented retaliation that independently qualifies under FCBA §1666
> and Visa Rule 11.3.4.
> A determination that ignores such evidence constitutes reckless disregard and negligent
> handling of my billing error claim.
> This evidence was not considered in Navy’s prior determination, which renders that
> decision inconsistent with FCBA §1666 and Visa Core Rules.
> Clarifications
> Audio vs. Written Evidence
> While I understand Navy may not process audio recordings directly, this dispute does
> not depend on audio. The controlling statutes (FTC Act §5, FCBA §1666) make any
> misrepresentation - oral, written, or implied - actionable. Even without audio, the
> time-stamped written record alone proves premature payment, nondisclosure, and
> retaliation. Audio merely corroborates what the written record already establishes. Even
> without audio, the written record alone proves premature payment, nondisclosure, and
> retaliation. Audio is corroboration, not the foundation of this claim.
> Portal Access ≠ Delivery
> Providing temporary portal access does not cure misrepresentation or nondelivery. Visa
> Core Rule 12.5.3.2 defines “services not provided” to include services that were
> materially misrepresented or later revoked.
> Access stripped in retaliation after a lawful review is not “delivery” - it is nondelivery and
> retaliation under both FCBA and Visa Rules.
> Delivery must align with advertised promises, not mere access to a portal. Temporary
> portal access or revoked services cannot cure misrepresentation or retaliation, and
> cannot be deemed “delivery” under Visa Rule 12.5.3.2.
> Merchant Policy Cannot Override Law
> Navy’s conclusion that “the merchant did not violate their policy” is misplaced. Visa Core
> Rule 5.4.2.5 requires refund terms to be disclosed before payment.
> My evidence proves payment was processed at 4:42 PM, contract/terms at 4:46 PM.
> Any “no-refund” policy presented post-payment is void. FCBA §1666 and Restatement
> (Second) of Contracts §17 make clear: no contract is enforceable without mutual assent
> before consideration. Reliance on the merchant’s internal refund policy cannot
> substitute for compliance with FCBA §1666 or Visa Core Rules.
> No $300K in Writing
> Even if the $300K in 90 days was communicated verbally, the merchant’s written ads
> (attached) still misrepresented material outcomes: “$300–500K funding at 0% interest.”
> FCBA and FTC Act §5 apply equally to verbal and written inducements.
> Misrepresentation does not require an exact dollar figure or timeframe - it requires that
> claims were misleading and likely to induce reliance, which is documented here.
> Whether or not the $300K timeline was in writing, the documented written
> misrepresentations (ads promising $300–500K at 0%) independently establish
> misrepresentation under Visa Rule 12.5.3.2 and FTC Act §5.
> 12323150.1.4-14
> Summary of Decisive Facts
> ● Payment was processed before terms were disclosed → contract defenses and
> refund policies are void.
> ● Portal access or “policy compliance” ≠ delivery under Visa Rule 12.5.3.2 when
> services are misrepresented or revoked.
> ● Written ads promising $300–500K at 0% establish misrepresentation under FTC
> Act §5, regardless of verbal promises.
> ● Audio evidence is not required - the time-stamped written record alone proves
> nondisclosure, misrepresentation, and retaliation.
> Closing
> Accordingly, Navy’s prior reliance on merchant policies, portal access, or absence of a
> written “90 days” promise does not negate the statutory and Visa Rule violations already
> proven. The evidence package already submitted exceeds the evidentiary burden
> under:
> ● FCBA §1666(a)(3)(B) (billing error: services not accepted or delivered as
> agreed),
> ● Visa Rule 11.3.4 (no suppression of chargeback rights),
> ● Visa Rule 12.5.3.2 (misrepresentation / nondelivery), and
> ● FTC Act §5 (unfair or deceptive acts).
> Any continued denial at this stage would constitute willful noncompliance, reckless
> disregard, and negligent handling of my billing error claim.
> This case is already documented under CFPB Case #240426-14545424,
> 250825-23483188, and FTC Report #190055885.
> I respectfully request immediate escalation to Navy’s legal/compliance team and
> Visa Compliance for correction and full chargeback approval.
> Thus, reliance on merchant policy and portal access as “delivery” is inconsistent with
> FCBA §1666, Visa Core Rules, and the evidence submitted.
> This case must be escalated for correction, as the prior conclusion constitutes negligent
> handling of a billing error claim.
> Accordingly, reliance on portal access, merchant policies, or the absence of a written
> “90 days” promise does not negate the statutory and Visa Rule violations already
> proven.
> Reliance on the merchant’s policy, disclosed post-payment, is void under FCBA
> §1666 and Visa Core Rule 5.4.2.5.
> Temporary portal access does not constitute delivery under Visa Rule 12.5.3.2
> where services were misrepresented and later revoked in retaliation.
> Whether or not the $300K timeline was in writing, the merchant’s written ads
> promising $300–500K funding at 0% independently establish misrepresentation
> under Visa Rule 12.5.3.2 and FTC Act §5.
> Even without audio, the written, time-stamped evidence alone establishes billing
> error, nondelivery, and retaliation.
> Thus, Navy’s prior determination was inconsistent with law and Visa Core Rules.
> Any continued denial would constitute willful noncompliance, reckless disregard,
> and negligent handling of my billing error claim.
> This case must be escalated to Navy’s legal/compliance team and Visa Compliance for
> correction and immediate full chargeback approval.
> Respectfully,
> Alan Nguyen
> 12323150.1.5-14
> I respectfully request immediate compliance review of a $20,600 transaction with Credit
> Counsel Elite (processed April 25, 2025). Navy Federal Credit Union has denied my
> chargeback based solely on the merchant’s “policy” and “portal access,” disregarding binding
> Visa Core Rules and federal law.
> Decisive Facts (Undisputed)
> 1. Payment Before Disclosure of Terms
> ○ Payment posted 4:42 PM. Contract containing no-refund clause sent 4:46
> PM.
> ○ Violations:
> ■ Visa Core Rule 5.4.2.5 - Refund/cancellation policies must be
> disclosed before payment.
> ■ Visa Core Rule 11.3.4 - No waiver/suppression of chargeback
> rights.
> ■ E-SIGN Act §7001(c)(1)(A) - Consent must follow disclosure.
> ■ Restatement (Second) of Contracts §17 - Mutual assent required
> before consideration.
> 2. Misrepresentation & Nondelivery
> ○ Ads promised $300K-$500K at 0% within 90 days.
> ○ $0 obtained through their program; portal access yielded no delivery of the
> promised outcome.
> ○ Violation: Visa Rule 12.5.3.2 - Misrepresentation/nondelivery includes
> materially false claims or revoked services.
> 3. Coercion & Retaliation
> ○ Merchant threatened blacklisting and revoked access after I pursued a
> dispute and posted a lawful review.
> ○ Violations:
> ■ Visa Rule 11.3.4 - No interference or retaliation against cardholder
> rights.
> ■ Consumer Review Fairness Act (15 U.S.C. §45b).
> ■ FTC Act §5 – Unfair/deceptive practices.
> Navy Federal’s Non-Compliance
> ● Navy’s denial rests only on the merchant’s refund policy and temporary portal access.
> ● This ignores Visa’s definitions: “delivery” requires promised services, not mere login
> access. Revocation after retaliation = nondelivery under Visa 12.5.3.2.
> ● Navy dismissed evidence because it was “audio,” but the time-stamped written
> record alone proves premature payment, nondisclosure, and misrepresentation.
> Audio merely corroborates.
> ● Navy failed to apply FCBA §1666(a)(3)(B), which defines billing errors as charges for
> services not accepted or delivered as agreed.
> Prior Regulatory Filings
> ● CFPB Case #240426-14545424 and #250825-23483188
> ● FTC Report #190055885
> Requested Action
> I respectfully request:
> 1. Enforced compliance with Navy Federal to process a full chargeback reversal of
> 2. 3. $20,600.
> Confirm that CCE’s contract/no-refund clause is void due to
> payment-before-disclosure and misrepresentation.
> Require Navy Federal to align its dispute handling with Visa Core Rules (5.4.2.5,
> 11.3.4, 12.5.3.2) and FCBA §1666.
> This case qualifies for manual exception processing under FCBA and Visa Core Rules. Navy
> Federal’s reliance on void merchant policies and portal access contradicts Visa’s standards and
> federal law.
> The only compliant resolution is a full chargeback and refund.
> Respectfully,
> Alan Nguyen
> Phone: 702-267-7169
> Email: nguyenalan95@gmail.com
> 12323150.1.6-14
> 1) SUMMARY
> I paid $20,600 to enroll in a high-ticket financial program called Credit Counsel
> Elite (CCE). The service was represented in multiple sales calls as a high-touch,
> done-for-you (DFY) mentorship experience, where I would receive personalized
> help to secure funding, restructure existing debt, and access urgent 1-on-1
> support with bank relationships and approvals.
> However, these core services were never delivered. Support was delayed,
> inconsistent, and failed to meet the agreed-upon scope. I received no viable
> funding solutions, and was left to self-direct the entire process. The only 1-on-1
> call came 12–14 days before a known funding deadline and offered no actionable
> resolution. No strategic execution or done-for-you deliverables were provided.
> Additionally, I was not presented with any contract, refund policy, or written
> terms at the time of payment. These were delivered after funds were collected,
> in violation of Restatement (Second) of Contracts §§17, 22, 211(3). This means
> no enforceable agreement was in place at the time of transaction— voiding
> any post-payment refund restrictions.
> Furthermore, CCE’s response to my refund request included threats of
> blacklisting me from financial institutions, and defamatory language implying
> criminal behavior — a potential violation of the Fair Credit Reporting Act (FCRA),
> the Equal Credit Opportunity Act (ECOA), and Federal Trade Commission
> (FTC) protections against deceptive practices.
> Per Visa Core Rules §11.3.4, no merchant may enforce a waiver of chargeback
> rights, and any such clause is void. I am exercising my right under this provision.
> I am filing this dispute based on:
> ● Material misrepresentation and omission during the sales process
> (Contract §164)
> ● Non-delivery of essential services
> ● Lack of mutual assent at time of transaction
> ● Unconscionable and one-sided contract terms (UCC §2-302)
> ● Post-payment refund terms, unenforceable under card network rules
> I request a full reversal of the $20,600 charge on the basis of non-receipt of
> services and post-payment misrepresentation.
> 2) PROOF OF PAYMENT TIME - FRIDAY APR 25, @**4:42PM**
> (BEFORE ANY CONTRACTS OR TERMS PROVIDED)
> 3) PDF CONTRACT PRESENTED **AFTER** PAYMENT - @**4:46PM**
> a. Lack of Mutual Assent at Time of Payment
> Protection: Restatement (Second) of Contracts §§17, 22, 211(3)
> A contract requires mutual consent at the time of the transaction. If you pay before seeing or
> agreeing to key terms (like refund policy or waivers), those terms are unenforceable.
> 12323150.1.7-14
> Paid before receiving the contract → no valid agreement.
> b. Voidability Due to Material Misrepresentation or Omission
> Protection: Restatement (Second) §164
> Induced by false promises, omissions, and/or misleading framing, rendering the contract voidable,
> even if signed.
> They sold the program as “done-for-you” and high-touch — then delivered delayed, hands-off support. That’s
> misrepresentation.
> c. Unconscionable Contract Terms
> Protection: UCC §2-302
> A contract is unenforceable if it’s “overly harsh, one-sided, or imposed unfairly.”
> A $30K “no refund” clause introduced after payment, paired with non-performance, is unconscionable.
> d. Visa and Mastercard Chargeback Rights
> Protection: Visa Core Rule §11.3.4 / Mastercard Chargeback Guidelines
> A merchant cannot require or enforce a waiver of chargeback rights. Any such clause is void and
> unenforceable by both law and card policy.
> They included language restricting refunds and chargebacks after one paid — it automatically voids that clause.
> e. FCRA & ECOA Protections (Against Retaliation)
> Protection: Fair Credit Reporting Act (FCRA) & Equal Credit Opportunity Act (ECOA)
> If they:
> ● Attempt to “blacklist” you from banks
> ● Threaten to interfere with your ability to get credit
> Constitutes:
> - Unlawful interference
> - Defamation
> - Violation of federal rights to fair access to credit
> f. Consumer Fraud and Deceptive Practices Protections
> Protection: Federal Trade Commission Act (FTC Act §5)
> It’s illegal to engage in unfair or deceptive acts in commerce — including misleading sales practices
> and refusal to refund in bad faith.
> Their misrepresentation and post-payment enforcement of refund denial violates this standard.
> 3) RETALIATION & ILLEGAL THREATS
> This message from the merchant shows a clear refusal to issue a refund and includes retaliatory
> threats, including blacklisting me from banks and equating my refund request with "terrorism."
> These actions violate consumer protection laws and card network policies, including:
> ● FCRA & ECOA – Attempting to interfere with access to credit through blacklisting
> ● FTC Act §5 – Unfair and deceptive business practices
> ● Visa Core Rule 11.3.4 – Prohibits merchants from restricting chargeback rights
> This communication demonstrates bad faith, non-delivery of promised services, and legal
> violations. Submitted as supporting documentation for chargeback review.
> “I will notify our bank connections that will therefore blacklist you from each of these banks.”
> “We don’t negotiate with terrorists.”
> I am exercising my right under consumer and payment protection law in good faith. I
> respectfully request that this transaction be reversed immediately to prevent further
> harm and financial exposure.

> What happened
> Nature: Misrepresentation, Non-Delivery, Retaliation, Refund Refusal, Unlawful Practices I am filing this complaint to report unlawful
> conduct by Credit Counsel Elite and its principal, Brandon Elliot. I paid $20,600 upfront based on their verbal and written claims that I
> would be obtaining $300,000+ in 0% business funding within 30-90 days. I clearly disclosed my unique financial circumstances in
> advance, and they assured me that I will confidently be able to achieve that goal given the specifics discussed. After payment was
> submitted, before any contract was sent or signed; there was a total failure of promised delivery. Key Facts: I received no actual
> funding, no tailored support, and no meaningful help aligned to my stated constraints. CCE’s “coaching” consisted of generic bank
> lists, outdated data, and DIY tasks I have already obtained online for free. They try to disguise this as "proprietary data" when it is
> offered freely online. Their "deeply discounted" “Authorized User” tradeline marketplace was falsely advertised - priced 2x higher
> than both public and private market rates. Their data caused multiple applications to be flagged, triggering unnecessary income
> verification - the opposite of what they guaranteed. Misleading. They offered no real funding partners, only a handful of alleged
> “RMs” (many unresponsive or ineffective). After asking for a refund, they illegally threatened to call the banks and blacklist me from
> services. When I posted a truthful public review, they illegally retaliated by revoking all access, cutting me off from anything I paid for.
> Documented FTC Violations (All Enforceable): FTC Act §5 (Deceptive Practices): - False and unsubstantiated claims about results -
> Bait-and-switch offers (e.g., deep AU discounts) - Misrepresenting capabilities and success metrics FTC Enforcement of FCBA (15
> U.S.C. §1666): - Illegally refusing refunds for undelivered services - “All sales final” language does not override federal law Negative
> Option Rule / ROSCA Violations: - Automatically enforcing terms without aﬃrmative, informed consent - Collecting payment prior to
> agreement delivery - Retaliation & Unfair Business Practices: - Revoking access after leaving a truthful, verified review - Retaliatory
> blacklisting and verbal discussion of collections and using their legal team Unlawful Contract Practices (Restatement Contracts §§17,
> 22; UCC §2-302): - Payment was made without mutual assent, rendering contract void - No real opportunity to negotiate or decline
> terms post-payment Why Refund Is Required Under FTC Authority: The FTC requires all representations be truthful, substantiated,
> and non-deceptive. Any contract is void if obtained through misrepresentation. “All sales final” is unenforceable when services are
> never rendered or fail to match advertised claims. CCE failed to provide any tangible deliverables matching their promises. I was
> misled, harmed financially, and retaliated against for speaking truthfully - a clear violation of both FTC and Visa/Mastercard policies
> prohibiting retaliation. Requested Remedy: I request a full $20,600 refund due everything above, incuding: Material

> The following references the complaint we received on 29 August 2025 from the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau on behalf of Navy Federal member Alan Nguyen. The complaint was assigned a case number of 250828-23580468. As we noted in our response to CFPB case number 250825-23483188, on 21 May 2025, our  member submitted a credit card merchant dispute claim in the amount of $20,600.00 against CREDIT COUNSEL ELITE. On 22 May 2025, our member requested to close the claim. On 21 July 2025, per our member’s request, we reopened the case and requested additional documentation. On 31 July 2025, we issued provisional credit and submitted a chargeback request to the merchant. On 25 August 2025, the merchant declined the chargeback and provided documentation supporting the charges. Subsequently, the claim was closed, and the provisional credit was reversed. On 11 September 2025, we reissued provisional credit and submitted a pre-arbitration request to the merchant, which was accepted. The claim was honored, and the provisional credit was made permanent.  On 31 July 2025, $8.51 of interest charges were credited to our member’s account. We have confirmed that we are reporting accurate information to the four nationwide credit reporting agencies regarding our member’s Navy Federal credit card account. We will not request the removal of valid data from our member’s credit file. Should our member have any questions, they can call us anytime toll-free at 1-888-842-NFCU (6328).

Subject: Final Pre-Litigation Demand - $20,600 Billing Error Dispute
Dear Navy Federal,
This letter serves as my final pre-litigation demand regarding Navy Federal’s mishandling of my
$20,600 dispute with Credit Counsel Elite. If this matter is not resolved on the terms below, I will
file suit in U.S. District Court on September 26, 2025 and pursue all remedies available under
the Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666), Regulation Z (12 C.F.R. §1026.13), the FTC Act,
state UDAP statutes, and Visa Core Rules.
Navy Federal has also failed to provide the required written explanation of its
denial, itself a direct violation of FCBA §1666 and Reg Z §1026.13(f).
Documented Violations by Navy Federal
1. Failure to Conduct Reasonable Investigation
○ Violation of FCBA §1666(a) and Reg Z §1026.13(c) by disregarding evidence:
payment processed at 4:42 PM before contract disclosure at 4:46 PM, fraudulent
$300K-$500K earnings inducements, and retaliatory revocation of access.
○ A form denial based solely on merchant policy is not a “reasonable investigation”
under the statute.
2. Unlawful Collection Activity During Dispute
12428080.1.2-6
○ Violation of FCBA §1666(a)(3)(B)(ii) by re-posting the $20,600 charge, accruing
interest, and reporting utilization while the billing error claim was unresolved.
3. Non-Delivery of Services
○ Violation of FCBA §1666(b)(3) where services were not delivered as agreed at
the time of the transaction. Payment was taken prior to disclosure, course
content instructed unlawful misrepresentation, and access was revoked in
retaliation.
4. Failure to Apply Visa Core Rules (12.5.3.2)
○ Navy Federal failed to honor mandatory chargeback rights for
misrepresentation/non-delivery, which apply regardless of merchant refund
policies or “portal access.”
5. Improper Deference to Merchant Policy Despite Fraud
○ By deferring to Credit Counsel Elite’s “no-refund” terms, Navy Federal is
effectively shielding a merchant engaged in fraudulent inducement and unlawful
training.
○ FCBA §1666 requires independent investigation; Visa rules override merchant
policies; and contracts formed under fraud and lack of mutual assent are void ab
initio.
6. Failure to Provide Written Explanation of Denial
● FCBA §1666(a)(3)(B): Requires creditors who deny a billing error to provide a written
explanation or clarification of reasons.
● Reg Z §1026.13(f): Requires issuers to mail/deliver that explanation and provide
supporting documents upon request.
● Navy Federal’s Failure: Navy’s response consisted only of a conclusory “no billing error
occurred,” with no reasoning and no engagement with the evidence provided.
● Conclusion: This omission is a direct statutory violation, further demonstrating willful
noncompliance and bad faith handling of the dispute.
Damages and Litigation Exposure
If litigation is required, I will seek:
● Refund of $20,600 - immediate billing correction.
● Statutory Damages: up to $10,000 under FCBA/Reg Z.
● Actual Damages: unlawful interest, credit harm, and consequential costs
($2,000–$10,000+).
● Punitive Damages: for willful mishandling and reckless disregard ($10,000–$20,000+).
● Attorney’s Fees & Costs: fully recoverable under FCBA if counsel is engaged.
Total Litigation Exposure: $67,000–$120,000+ in addition to the $20,600 refund, plus
reputational risk, regulatory referral, and Visa compliance penalties.
Settlement Proposal

Waiver of all accrued interest and fees.
Correction of any adverse credit reporting/utilization.
Additional immediate $10,000 in settlement for statutory damages, punitive exposure,
and the hardship, stress, and time expended due to Navy Federal’s mishandling.
Written assurance of no retaliation – no closure, restriction, or adverse action against my
Navy Federal accounts or membership for exercising my rights under FCBA and Reg Z.

Attachment A - Navy Federal Rebuttal
Matrix
Subject: Anticipated Defenses and Legal Responses
Reference: $20,600 Billing Error Dispute – Credit Counsel Elite
This matrix outlines likely defenses Navy Federal may raise and the corresponding multi-angle
legal responses. Each defense is nullified under Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666),
Regulation Z (12 C.F.R. §1026.13), the FTC Act (15 U.S.C. §45), state UDAP statutes, and Visa
Core Rules (12.5.3.2).
1. “Portal access = delivery”
Why It Fails:
● FCBA §1666(b)(3): Services not delivered as agreed at the time of the transaction =
billing error.
● Visa Rule 12.5.3.2: Chargebacks required for misrepresentation or non-delivery
regardless of portal access.
● FTC Act §45(a): Unlawful/deceptive services = non-delivery.
● Retaliation: Revoked access proves no bona fide delivery.
Conclusion: Under FCBA §1666(b)(3) and Visa Rule 12.5.3.2, portal access—especially when
later revoked or unlawful—cannot constitute delivery.
2. “You initialed the contract = consent”
Why It Fails:
● Timeline Evidence: Payment 4:42 PM, terms disclosed 4:46 PM.
● Restatement (Second) Contracts §17 & UCC §2-204(1): Mutual assent must exist at
formation.
● Fraudulent Inducement: Earnings misrepresentation voids contract.
Conclusion: Because payment preceded disclosure, there was no contemporaneous assent.
Under Restatement §17 and UCC §2-204(1), retroactive initials do not create assent. The
contract is void for lack of mutual assent.
3. “No refund policy applies”
Why It Fails:
● FCBA §1666(a): Issuer must investigate billing errors regardless of merchant policy.
● Visa Rule 12.5.3.2: Overrides refund/cancellation terms.
● FTC Act §45(a): Retaining funds obtained by deception is unlawful.
● Contract/Public Policy: Fraud vitiates refund restrictions.
Conclusion: Merchant policies cannot override FCBA §1666 or Visa Rule 12.5.3.2. A no-refund
clause tied to a fraudulent contract is unenforceable as against public policy.
4. “We relied on the merchant’s confirmation”
Why It Fails:
● FCBA §1666(a): Requires issuer to perform a “reasonable investigation,” not adopt
merchant statements.
● Reg Z §1026.13(f): Must provide a written explanation addressing the consumer’s
evidence.
● CFPB/NCUA Guidance: Merchant reliance = noncompliance.
12428080.1.5-6
Conclusion: Blind reliance violates FCBA §1666(a) and Reg Z §1026.13(f). Issuers must
conduct an independent investigation; failure is a statutory violation.
5. “You completed the course content”
Why It Fails:
● FTC Act §45(a): Unlawful training = non-delivery.
● FCBA §1666(b)(3): Services premised on misrepresentation are “not delivered as
agreed.”
● Contract Law: Fraudulent inducement voids assent.
● Retaliation: Revoked access = non-delivery.
Conclusion: Under FCBA §1666(b)(3) and FTC Act §45(a), unlawful or retaliatory services
cannot constitute valid delivery, regardless of completion.
6. “We followed proper procedure”
Why It Fails:
● Reg Z §1026.13(c): Requires acknowledgment of billing error notice.
● Reg Z §1026.13(f): Requires substantive written explanation.
● FCBA §1666(a)(3)(B)(ii): Prohibits collection during pending dispute.
Conclusion: If Navy’s “procedure” resulted in collection during dispute and reliance on merchant
policy, it is noncompliant by definition under FCBA §1666 and Reg Z §1026.13.
7. “This is not a billing error”
Why It Fails:
● FCBA §1666(b)(3): Defines billing errors to include when “services were not delivered as
agreed.”
● Evidence: Premature payment, misrepresentation, retaliation = textbook billing error.
● Visa Rule 12.5.3.2: Chargebacks required for misrepresentation/non-delivery regardless
of merchant stance.
Conclusion: The transaction falls squarely within FCBA §1666(b)(3) and Visa Rule 12.5.3.2.
Navy Federal’s contrary position would be legally indefensible.
Closing Statement
Each potential defense fails under multiple, overlapping legal authorities. Navy Federal cannot
rely on merchant policies, portal access, initials, or internal procedures to override statutory
protections. The only compliant resolution is:
● Refund of $20,600,
● Waiver of all interest/fees,
● Correction of utilization/credit reporting,
● Additional damages settlement, and
● Written no-retaliation assurance.

Statutory Basis & Issuer Exposure (Read First)
This matter arises from First Interstate Bank’s mishandling of a federally protected billing error dispute, in violation of the Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666), Regulation Z (12 C.F.R. §1026.13), and federal UDAAP standards enforced by the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau.
Once First Interstate Bank received notice of a billing error, the bank assumed independent, non-delegable statutory duties to:
• Conduct a reasonable investigation (§1666(a); Reg Z §1026.13(c))
 • Refrain from collection or adverse action during the dispute (§1666(a)(3)(B)(ii))
 • Provide a written explanation of any denial addressing the consumer’s evidence (§1666(a)(3)(B); Reg Z §1026.13(f))
 • Avoid reliance on merchant assertions or unenforceable policies
Failure to meet these duties exposes the issuer to statutory damages, actual damages, attorney’s fees, regulatory enforcement, supervisory findings, and civil money penalties. These obligations apply regardless of merchant refund policies, alleged service hours, or internal merchant narratives.
PART I - PLAIN-ENGLISH EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
(this frames everything that follows)
This dispute is not just “services not rendered.”
 It is a pattern of misrepresentation, lack of agreement, retroactive billing, and procedural unfairness, compounded by the bank’s failure to conduct a reasonable investigation. There is a pattern of conduct that, once notice was provided, that supports findings of willful non-compliance, willful negligence, willful misconduct, and reckless disregard.
What actually happened - in simple terms:
I purchased a Done-For-You (DFY) Airbnb service, NOT consulting.
The merchant promised (verified in already provided documentation: ads, website, sales calls, written materials, graphics, and transcripts) to:


Acquire a property
Furnish it
List it
Deliver a cash-flowing Airbnb generating ~$3,500-$5,000+/month
With explicit representations such as: “If we don’t acquire you a property, you pay $0.”
Explicit week by week timeline of deliverables 


None of that happened. (all proven and verified factually and indisputably via already provided ads, website, sales calls, written materials, graphics, and transcripts)


No property acquired
No lease signed
No furnishing
No listing
No revenue


After failing to deliver, the merchant changed the story:
They retroactively claimed the money was for “consulting”
They alleged 109 hours at $45/hour to justify retaining $10,500 (unsupported merchant allegation; unproven, undocumented, and never consented to) - notwithstanding that the merchant only schedules optional weekly calls of approximately one hour.
The asserted “109 hours at $45/hour” figure is not a term agreed to by the consumer, nor is it supported by any executed agreement, disclosure, or consent. This figure is an unsupported allegation made by the merchant and relayed by the bank, introduced only after the merchant failed to deliver the promised Done-For-You Airbnb service. At no point did the consumer agree to hourly consulting, time-based billing, or any pricing structure contingent on hours worked rather than outcome delivered. The consumer’s actual participation consisted of occasional weekly update calls of approximately one hour, which were ancillary to the promised DFY service and never represented or disclosed as billable consulting.

This hourly billing:


Was never disclosed
Was never agreed to
Was never signed
Was never part of the deal


In reality, I only had occasional weekly calls (~1 hour each), which were calls to keep me up to date, not paid hourly consulting.

Under FTC Act §5, the transaction is defined by the net impression of the merchant’s advertising and sales representations. 
All representations in this matter concerned a Done-For-You Airbnb acquisition and handover, not hourly consulting. 
The merchant’s post-hoc claim of ‘109 hours at $45/hour’ was never disclosed, never agreed to, and is materially inconsistent with the advertised deliverables, including express representations that no payment would be owed absent property acquisition. 
(unsupported merchant allegation; no signed agreement, disclosure, or consumer consent exists)

The FTC treats such retroactive recharacterization as deceptive, unreasonable, and unenforceable. Reliance on unsigned or undisclosed terms cannot cure or override deceptive advertising.

These FTC Act §5 standards are directly applicable to First Interstate Bank as the issuer responsible for conducting a reasonable investigation and for avoiding unfair or deceptive practices in dispute resolution. 
Once the bank is placed on notice that a merchant’s post-transaction narrative is materially inconsistent with the net impression of the advertising and sales representations, the bank may not lawfully rely on that narrative to deny a billing error claim. 
Accepting and enforcing an undisclosed, unsigned, and retroactively imposed billing theory, particularly one that contradicts express outcome-based promises; exposes the bank to liability for facilitating or perpetuating an unfair practice, failing to conduct a reasonable investigation, and improperly shifting the burden of proof to the consumer. 
Federal regulators, including the CFPB, evaluate such conduct as an independent compliance failure, regardless of merchant misconduct, where the issuer disregards clear evidence of deceptive inducement, lack of assent, and non-delivery. 
Continued enforcement after notice materially increases regulatory exposure, corrective action risk, restitution obligations, and supervisory findings.


Once First Interstate Bank was placed on actual notice of the material misrepresentations, lack of mutual assent, unenforceable and unsigned terms, and total non-delivery of the promised Done-For-You Airbnb service, the bank assumed an independent legal obligation to correct the billing error and conduct a reasonable, evidence-based investigation. At that point, continued reliance on the merchant’s post-hoc narrative, particularly one that contradicts the net impression of the merchant’s own advertising and sales representations - constitutes a separate and independent compliance failure by the issuer. 
Federal regulators do not permit issuers to defer to merchant assertions where the record demonstrates deceptive inducement, non-delivery, and lack of consumer consent. Continued enforcement after notice exposes the bank to issuer-side liability under Regulation Z, UDAAP standards enforced by the CFPB, and unfair-practice facilitation principles recognized under the FTC Act, regardless of whether the merchant itself is separately pursued.

There is no enforceable agreement supporting the merchant’s claims:
No signed contract
No DocuSign
No click-wrap
No assent
No meeting of the minds
No agreement authorizing hourly billing
No agreement allowing changed terms
No agreement making the transaction non-refundable


Because no executed agreement exists authorizing hourly consulting, retroactive billing, modified deliverables, or non-refundability, the merchant’s asserted “109 hours at $45/hour” theory is a legal nullity. Under basic contract law principles, a party cannot impose material pricing terms, scope changes, or billing structures absent mutual assent. An unsigned, undisclosed, or unaccepted document - whether characterized as “terms,” “policies,” or “internal agreements” - has no legal force and cannot be enforced by the merchant or adopted by the issuer. Any attempt to retain funds on this basis constitutes retention without contractual or legal authority.

Despite this, the bank:
Enforced unsigned terms
Shifted the burden of proof to the consumer
Accepted merchant allegations without proof
Denied access to rebuttal documents
Gave the merchant months to respond
Gave the consumer less than 10 days to respond - via a letter never received


The denial was not based on evidence of delivery.
 It was based on procedural technicalities and an unenforceable merchant narrative.
Maintaining denial under these circumstances, where the issuer has been provided overwhelming, documented evidence of deceptive inducement, non-delivery, lack of assent, and unenforceable terms - creates escalating regulatory exposure for First Interstate Bank. 
Under CFPB enforcement authority, issuers that engage in or perpetuate unfair, deceptive, or unreasonable dispute-resolution practices may be subject to restitution orders, corrective action mandates, supervisory findings, and civil money penalties. 
CFPB civil penalty tiers currently authorize fines of up to $5,000 per day for standard violations, up to $25,000 per day for reckless violations, and up to $1,000,000 per day for knowing violations, with exposure increasing once an institution has actual notice and continues the challenged conduct. 
Administrative correction at this stage is the only resolution consistent with regulatory expectations and risk management principles.

4. FTC Act §5 (15 U.S.C. §45)
 • Liability for facilitating or perpetuating unfair or deceptive practices once on notice
 • Issuer responsibility where enforcement enables merchant deception
Based on the documented timeline, repeated notice, and continued enforcement, First Interstate Bank’s exposure substantially exceeds the amount in dispute. Correction at this stage materially reduces regulatory, litigation, and reputational risk.

Statutory Basis & Issuer Exposure (Read First)
This matter arises from First Interstate Bank’s mishandling of a federally protected billing error dispute, in violation of the Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666), Regulation Z (12 C.F.R. §1026.13), and federal UDAAP standards enforced by the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau.
Once First Interstate Bank received notice of a billing error, the bank assumed independent, non-delegable statutory duties to:
• Conduct a reasonable investigation (§1666(a); Reg Z §1026.13(c))
 • Refrain from collection or adverse action during the dispute (§1666(a)(3)(B)(ii))
 • Provide a written explanation of any denial addressing the consumer’s evidence (§1666(a)(3)(B); Reg Z §1026.13(f)) and provide supporting documentation upon request.
 • Avoid reliance on merchant assertions or unenforceable policies
Failure to meet these duties exposes the issuer to statutory damages, actual damages, attorney’s fees, regulatory enforcement, supervisory findings, and civil money penalties. These obligations apply regardless of merchant refund policies, alleged service hours, or internal merchant narratives.
PART I - PLAIN-ENGLISH EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
(this frames everything that follows)
This dispute is not just “services not rendered.”
 It is a pattern of misrepresentation, lack of agreement, retroactive billing, and procedural unfairness, compounded by the bank’s failure to conduct a reasonable investigation. There is a pattern of conduct that, once notice was provided, supports findings of willful non-compliance, willful negligence, willful misconduct, and reckless disregard.
What actually happened - in simple terms:
I purchased a Done-For-You (DFY) Airbnb service, NOT consulting.
The merchant promised (verified in already provided documentation: ads, website, sales calls, written materials, graphics, and transcripts) to:


Acquire a property
Furnish it
List it
Deliver a cash-flowing Airbnb generating ~$3,500-$5,000+/month
With explicit representations such as: “If we don’t acquire you a property, you pay $0.”
Explicit week by week timeline of deliverables 


None of that happened. (all proven and verified factually and indisputably via already provided ads, website, sales calls, written materials, graphics, and transcripts)


No property acquired
No lease signed
No furnishing
No listing
No revenue


After failing to deliver, the merchant changed the story:
They retroactively claimed the money was for “consulting”
They alleged 109 hours at $45/hour to justify retaining $10,500 (unsupported merchant allegation; unproven, undocumented, and never consented to) - notwithstanding that the consumer’s participation consisted of occasional, optionally scheduled weekly calls of approximately one hour.
The asserted “109 hours at $45/hour” figure is not a term agreed to by the consumer, nor is it supported by any executed agreement, disclosure, or consent. This figure is an unsupported allegation made by the merchant and relayed by the bank, introduced only after the merchant failed to deliver the promised Done-For-You Airbnb service. At no point did the consumer agree to hourly consulting, time-based billing, or any pricing structure contingent on hours worked rather than outcome delivered. The consumer’s actual participation consisted of occasional weekly update calls of approximately one hour, which were ancillary to the promised DFY service and never represented or disclosed as billable consulting.

This hourly billing:


Was never disclosed
Was never agreed to
Was never signed
Was never part of the deal


In reality, I only had occasional weekly calls (~1 hour each), which were calls to keep me up to date, not paid hourly consulting.

Under FTC Act §5, the transaction is defined by the net impression of the merchant’s advertising and sales representations. 
All representations in this matter concerned a Done-For-You Airbnb acquisition and handover, not hourly consulting. 
The merchant’s post-hoc claim of ‘109 hours at $45/hour’ was never disclosed, never agreed to, and is materially inconsistent with the advertised deliverables, including express representations that no payment would be owed absent property acquisition. (unsupported merchant allegation; no signed agreement, disclosure, or consumer consent exists)

The FTC treats such retroactive recharacterization as deceptive, unreasonable, and unenforceable. Reliance on unsigned or undisclosed terms cannot cure or override deceptive advertising.

These FTC Act §5 standards are directly applicable to First Interstate Bank as the issuer responsible for conducting a reasonable investigation and for avoiding unfair or deceptive practices in dispute resolution. 
Once the bank is placed on notice that a merchant’s post-transaction narrative is materially inconsistent with the net impression of the advertising and sales representations, the bank may not lawfully rely on that narrative to deny a billing error claim. 
Accepting and enforcing an undisclosed, unsigned, and retroactively imposed billing theory, particularly one that contradicts express outcome-based promises; exposes the bank to liability for facilitating or perpetuating an unfair practice, failing to conduct a reasonable investigation, and improperly shifting the burden of proof to the consumer. 
Federal regulators, including the CFPB, evaluate such conduct as an independent compliance failure, regardless of merchant misconduct, where the issuer disregards clear evidence of deceptive inducement, lack of assent, and non-delivery. 
Continued enforcement after notice materially increases regulatory exposure, corrective action risk, restitution obligations, and supervisory findings.


Once First Interstate Bank was placed on actual notice of the material misrepresentations, lack of mutual assent, unenforceable and unsigned terms, and total non-delivery of the promised Done-For-You Airbnb service, the bank assumed an independent legal obligation to correct the billing error and conduct a reasonable, evidence-based investigation. At that point, continued reliance on the merchant’s post-hoc narrative, particularly one that contradicts the net impression of the merchant’s own advertising and sales representations - constitutes a separate and independent compliance failure by the issuer. 
Federal regulators do not permit issuers to defer to merchant assertions where the record demonstrates deceptive inducement, non-delivery, and lack of consumer consent. Continued enforcement after notice exposes the bank to issuer-side liability under Regulation Z, UDAAP standards enforced by the CFPB, and unfair-practice facilitation principles recognized under the FTC Act, regardless of whether the merchant itself is separately pursued.

There is no enforceable agreement supporting the merchant’s claims:
No signed contract
No DocuSign
No click-wrap
No assent
No meeting of the minds
No agreement authorizing hourly billing
No agreement allowing changed terms
No agreement making the transaction non-refundable


Because no executed agreement exists authorizing hourly consulting, retroactive billing, modified deliverables, or non-refundability, the merchant’s asserted “109 hours at $45/hour” theory is a legal nullity. Under basic contract law principles, a party cannot impose material pricing terms, scope changes, or billing structures absent mutual assent. An unsigned, undisclosed, or unaccepted document - whether characterized as “terms,” “policies,” or “internal agreements” - has no legal force and cannot be enforced by the merchant or adopted by the issuer. Any attempt to retain funds on this basis constitutes retention without contractual or legal authority.

Despite this, the bank:
Enforced unsigned terms
Shifted the burden of proof to the consumer
Accepted merchant allegations without proof
Denied access to rebuttal documents
Gave the merchant months to respond
Gave the consumer less than 10 days to respond - via a letter never received


The denial was not based on evidence of delivery.
 It was based on procedural technicalities and an unenforceable merchant narrative.
Maintaining denial under these circumstances, where the issuer has been provided overwhelming, documented evidence of deceptive inducement, non-delivery, lack of assent, and unenforceable terms - creates escalating regulatory exposure for First Interstate Bank. 
Under CFPB enforcement authority, issuers that engage in or perpetuate unfair, deceptive, or unreasonable dispute-resolution practices may be subject to restitution orders, corrective action mandates, supervisory findings, and civil money penalties. 
CFPB civil penalty tiers currently authorize fines of up to $5,000 per day for standard violations, up to $25,000 per day for reckless violations, and up to $1,000,000 per day for knowing violations, with exposure increasing once an institution has actual notice and continues the challenged conduct. 
Administrative correction at this stage is the only resolution consistent with regulatory expectations and risk management principles.
Comparable issuer-side correction after escalation has been granted in prior CFPB-supervised billing error matters where provisional credit was ultimately made permanent following issuer escalation.

Issuer Liability, Penalties, and Litigation Exposure
If First Interstate Bank continues to enforce an unenforceable merchant narrative after notice, the bank faces escalating exposure under multiple independent authorities, including but not limited to:
1. Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666)
 • Statutory damages as authorized under the Truth in Lending Act and Fair Credit Billing Act civil liability provisions (15 U.S.C. §1640), together with actual damages, costs, and mandatory attorney’s fees.
 • Actual damages (interest, fees, credit harm, consequential loss)
 • Mandatory attorney’s fees and costs
2. Regulation Z (12 C.F.R. §1026.13)
 • Civil liability for failure to investigate
 • Liability for defective notice and explanation
 • Liability for unlawful collection activity during dispute
3. CFPB UDAAP Authority (12 U.S.C. §§5531–5536)
 Civil money penalties of:
 - Up to $5,000 per day (standard violations)
 - Up to $25,000 per day (reckless violations)
 -  Up to $1,000,000 per day (knowing violations)
 - Restitution orders
 - Corrective action mandates
 - Supervisory findings and compliance downgrades
4. FTC Act §5 (15 U.S.C. §45)
 • Liability for facilitating or perpetuating unfair or deceptive practices once on notice
 • Issuer responsibility where enforcement enables merchant deception
Based on the documented timeline, repeated notice, and continued enforcement, First Interstate Bank’s exposure substantially exceeds the amount in dispute. Correction at this stage materially reduces regulatory, litigation, and reputational risk.

This is why this matter involves:
Fraudulent inducement
Material misrepresentation
Lack of mutual assent
Retroactive billing
Unfair and unreasonable investigation practices
Willful or reckless disregard once notice was provided
Willful non-compliance
Willful misconduct

The consumer is not required to disprove unsupported allegations or reconstruct a transaction that was never agreed to. # I. STATUTORY BASIS & ISSUER EXPOSURE (READ FIRST)

This matter arises from First Interstate Bank’s mishandling of a federally protected billing error dispute, in violation of the **Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666)**, **Regulation Z (12 C.F.R. §1026.13)**, and federal **UDAAP** standards enforced by the **Consumer Financial Protection Bureau (CFPB)**.

## I.A. Trigger Event — Notice of Billing Error

Once First Interstate Bank received notice of a billing error, the bank assumed **independent, non-delegable statutory duties**.

## I.B. Non-Delegable Issuer Duties Upon Notice

1. **Conduct a reasonable investigation**  
   (15 U.S.C. §1666(a); 12 C.F.R. §1026.13(c))

2. **Refrain from collection or adverse action during the dispute**  
   (15 U.S.C. §1666(a)(3)(B)(ii))

3. **Provide a written explanation of any denial addressing the consumer’s evidence and provide supporting documentation upon request**  
   (15 U.S.C. §1666(a)(3)(B); 12 C.F.R. §1026.13(f))

4. **Avoid reliance on merchant assertions or unenforceable policies**  
   (Issuer compliance obligation under FCBA, Regulation Z, and UDAAP standards)

## I.C. Consequences of Non-Compliance

Failure to meet these duties exposes the issuer to **statutory damages**, **actual damages**, **mandatory attorney’s fees**, **regulatory enforcement**, **supervisory findings**, and **civil money penalties**. These obligations apply **regardless of merchant refund policies, alleged service hours, or internal merchant narratives**.

---

# II. PLAIN-ENGLISH EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

## II.A. Executive Frame — What This Dispute Is

This dispute is not merely “services not rendered.”  
It is a **pattern of misrepresentation, lack of agreement, retroactive billing, and procedural unfairness**, compounded by the bank’s failure to conduct a reasonable investigation. Once notice was provided, the conduct supports findings of **willful non-compliance, willful negligence, willful misconduct, and reckless disregard**.

## II.B. What Was Purchased

I purchased a **Done-For-You (DFY) Airbnb service**, **not consulting**.

## II.C. What Was Promised (Documented Scope)

The merchant promised — as verified in already provided documentation (ads, website, sales calls, written materials, graphics, and transcripts):

1. Acquisition of a property  
2. Furnishing of the property  
3. Listing of the property  
4. Delivery of a cash-flowing Airbnb generating approximately **$3,500–$5,000+ per month**  
5. An explicit **week-by-week timeline of deliverables**  
6. An express risk-reversal representation: **“If we don’t acquire you a property, you pay $0.”**

## II.D. What Was Delivered (Binary Facts)

None of the promised deliverables occurred, as proven factually and indisputably by the record:

1. No property acquired  
2. No lease signed  
3. No furnishing  
4. No listing  
5. No revenue

## II.E. Merchant’s Post-Failure Recharacterization (109 Hours / $45 per Hour)

After failing to deliver, the merchant changed the story:

- Retroactively claimed the payment was for “consulting”
- Alleged **109 hours at $45/hour** to justify retaining **$10,500**  
  *(unsupported merchant allegation; unproven, undocumented, and never consented to)*

The consumer’s participation consisted only of **occasional, optionally scheduled weekly calls of approximately one hour**.

### Key Defects in the “109 Hours” Claim

1. Never disclosed pre-sale  
2. Never agreed to  
3. Never signed or executed  
4. No contemporaneous time records, invoices, or consumer-acknowledged logs produced  
5. Inconsistent with actual participation (occasional ~1-hour update calls)

At no point did the consumer agree to hourly consulting, time-based billing, or pricing contingent on hours worked rather than **outcome delivered**.

## II.F. Why the 109-Hour Theory Fails Legally

### II.F.1. FTC Act §5 — Net Impression Controls

Under **FTC Act §5**, the transaction is defined by the **net impression** of the merchant’s advertising and sales representations.  
All representations concerned a **DFY Airbnb acquisition and handover**, not hourly consulting.  

The merchant’s post-hoc claim of “109 hours at $45/hour” was never disclosed, never agreed to, and is materially inconsistent with the advertised deliverables, including express representations that no payment would be owed absent property acquisition *(unsupported merchant allegation; no signed agreement, disclosure, or consumer consent exists)*.

The FTC treats such retroactive recharacterization as **deceptive, unreasonable, and unenforceable**. Reliance on unsigned or undisclosed terms cannot cure or override deceptive advertising.

### II.F.2. Contract Formation — No Mutual Assent

There is **no enforceable agreement** supporting the merchant’s claims:

- No signed contract  
- No DocuSign  
- No click-wrap  
- No assent  
- No meeting of the minds  
- No agreement authorizing hourly billing  
- No agreement allowing changed terms  
- No agreement making the transaction non-refundable  

Because no executed agreement exists authorizing hourly consulting, retroactive billing, modified deliverables, or non-refundability, the merchant’s asserted “109 hours at $45/hour” theory is a **legal nullity**. An unsigned, undisclosed, or unaccepted document has **no legal force** and cannot be enforced by the merchant or adopted by the issuer.

## II.G. Issuer Accountability After Notice

These FTC Act §5 standards apply directly to **First Interstate Bank** as the issuer responsible for conducting a reasonable investigation and avoiding unfair or deceptive practices in dispute resolution.

Once the bank was placed on notice that the merchant’s narrative was materially inconsistent with the net impression of the advertising and sales representations, the bank **may not lawfully rely on that narrative** to deny a billing error claim.

Issuer obligations under the FCBA and Regulation Z are **non-delegable** and cannot be satisfied by deferring to merchant assertions where the evidentiary record contradicts those assertions.

## II.H. Procedural Unfairness and Defective Notice

Despite overwhelming evidence, the bank:

- Enforced unsigned terms  
- Shifted the burden of proof to the consumer  
- Accepted merchant allegations without proof  
- Denied access to rebuttal documents  
- Gave the merchant months to respond  
- Gave the consumer **less than 10 days** to respond via a letter **never received**

The denial was not based on evidence of delivery, but on **procedural technicalities and an unenforceable merchant narrative**.

## II.I. Correction Is the Only Compliant Outcome

Maintaining denial under these circumstances creates escalating regulatory exposure. Administrative correction is the **only resolution consistent with regulatory expectations and risk management principles**. Comparable issuer-side correction has been granted in prior CFPB-supervised billing error matters where provisional credit was ultimately made permanent following issuer escalation.

---

# III. ISSUER LIABILITY, PENALTIES, AND LITIGATION EXPOSURE

If First Interstate Bank continues to enforce an unenforceable merchant narrative after notice, exposure arises under multiple independent authorities:

## III.A. Fair Credit Billing Act / TILA (15 U.S.C. §§1666, 1640)

- Statutory damages as authorized under civil liability provisions  
- Actual damages (interest, fees, credit harm, consequential loss)  
- Mandatory attorney’s fees and costs

## III.B. Regulation Z (12 C.F.R. §1026.13)

- Civil liability for failure to investigate  
- Liability for defective notice and explanation  
- Liability for unlawful collection activity during dispute

## III.C. CFPB UDAAP Authority (12 U.S.C. §§5531–5536)

- Civil money penalties:  
  - Up to **$5,000/day** (standard violations)  
  - Up to **$25,000/day** (reckless violations)  
  - Up to **$1,000,000/day** (knowing violations)  
- Restitution orders  
- Corrective action mandates  
- Supervisory findings and compliance downgrades

## III.D. FTC Act §5 (15 U.S.C. §45)

- Liability for facilitating or perpetuating unfair or deceptive practices once on notice  
- Issuer responsibility where enforcement enables merchant deception

**Conclusion:** Based on the documented timeline and repeated notice, First Interstate Bank’s exposure substantially exceeds the amount in dispute. Correction materially reduces regulatory, litigation, and reputational risk.

VI. Preservation / Litigation Hold
VII. Final Note


I. STATUTORY BASIS & ISSUER EXPOSURE (READ FIRST)
This matter arises from First Interstate Bank’s mishandling of a federally protected billing error dispute, in violation of the Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666), Regulation Z (12 C.F.R. §1026.13), and federal UDAAP standards enforced by the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau (CFPB).

I.A. Trigger Event - Notice of Billing Error
Once First Interstate Bank received notice of a billing error, the bank assumed independent, non-delegable statutory duties.

I.B. Non-Delegable Issuer Duties Upon Notice
Conduct a reasonable investigation
 (15 U.S.C. §1666(a); 12 C.F.R. §1026.13(c))


Refrain from collection or adverse action during the dispute
 (15 U.S.C. §1666(a)(3)(B)(ii))


Provide a written explanation of any denial addressing the consumer’s evidence and provide supporting documentation upon request
 (15 U.S.C. §1666(a)(3)(B); 12 C.F.R. §1026.13(f))


Avoid reliance on merchant assertions or unenforceable policies
 (Issuer compliance obligation under FCBA, Regulation Z, and UDAAP standards)

I.C. Consequences of Non-Compliance
Failure to meet these duties exposes the issuer to statutory damages, actual damages, mandatory attorney’s fees, regulatory enforcement, supervisory findings, and civil money penalties. These obligations apply regardless of merchant refund policies, alleged service hours, or internal merchant narratives.

II. PLAIN-ENGLISH EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

II.A. Executive Frame - What This Dispute Is
This dispute is not merely “services not rendered.”
 It is a pattern of misrepresentation, lack of agreement, retroactive billing, and procedural unfairness, compounded by the bank’s failure to conduct a reasonable investigation. Once notice was provided, the conduct supports findings of willful non-compliance, willful negligence, willful misconduct, and reckless disregard.

II.B. What Was Purchased
I purchased a Done-For-You (DFY) Airbnb service, not consulting.

II.C. What Was Promised (Documented Scope)
The merchant promised — as verified in already provided documentation (ads, website, sales calls, written materials, graphics, and transcripts):
Acquisition of a property
Furnishing of the property
Listing of the property
Delivery of a cash-flowing Airbnb generating approximately $3,500–$5,000+ per month
An explicit week-by-week timeline of deliverables
An express risk-reversal representation: “If we don’t acquire you a property, you pay $0.”

II.D. What Was Delivered (Binary Facts)
None of the promised deliverables occurred, as proven factually and indisputably by the record:
No property acquired
No lease signed
No furnishing
No listing
No revenue

II.E. Merchant’s Post-Failure Recharacterization (109 Hours / $45 per Hour)
After failing to deliver, the merchant changed the story:
Retroactively claimed the payment was for “consulting”


Alleged 109 hours at $45/hour to justify retaining $10,500
 (unsupported merchant allegation; unproven, undocumented, and never consented to)


The consumer’s participation consisted only of occasional, optionally scheduled weekly calls of approximately one hour.
Key Defects in the “109 Hours” Claim
Never disclosed pre-sale
Never agreed to
Never signed or executed
No contemporaneous time records, invoices, or consumer-acknowledged logs produced


Inconsistent with actual participation (occasional ~1-hour update calls)


At no point did the consumer agree to hourly consulting, time-based billing, or pricing contingent on hours worked rather than outcome delivered.

II.F. Why the 109-Hour Theory Fails Legally

II.F.1. FTC Act §5 - Net Impression Controls
Under FTC Act §5, the transaction is defined by the net impression of the merchant’s advertising and sales representations.


All representations concerned a DFY Airbnb acquisition and handover, not hourly consulting.
The merchant’s post-hoc claim of “109 hours at $45/hour” was never disclosed, never agreed to, and is materially inconsistent with the advertised deliverables, including express representations that no payment would be owed absent property acquisition (unsupported merchant allegation; no signed agreement, disclosure, or consumer consent exists).
The FTC treats such retroactive recharacterization as deceptive, unreasonable, and unenforceable. Reliance on unsigned or undisclosed terms cannot cure or override deceptive advertising.
II.F.2. Contract Formation - No Mutual Assent
There is no enforceable agreement supporting the merchant’s claims:
No signed contract
No DocuSign
No click-wrap
No assent
No meeting of the minds
No agreement authorizing hourly billing
No agreement allowing changed terms
No agreement making the transaction non-refundable


Because no executed agreement exists authorizing hourly consulting, retroactive billing, modified deliverables, or non-refundability, the merchant’s asserted “109 hours at $45/hour” theory is a legal nullity. An unsigned, undisclosed, or unaccepted document has no legal force and cannot be enforced by the merchant or adopted by the issuer.

II.G. Issuer Accountability After Notice
These FTC Act §5 standards apply directly to First Interstate Bank as the issuer responsible for conducting a reasonable investigation and avoiding unfair or deceptive practices in dispute resolution.
Once the bank was placed on notice that the merchant’s narrative was materially inconsistent with the net impression of the advertising and sales representations, the bank may not lawfully rely on that narrative to deny a billing error claim.
Issuer obligations under the FCBA and Regulation Z are non-delegable and cannot be satisfied by deferring to merchant assertions where the evidentiary record contradicts those assertions.

II.H. Procedural Unfairness and Defective Notice
Despite overwhelming evidence, the bank:
Enforced unsigned terms
Shifted the burden of proof to the consumer
Accepted merchant allegations without proof
Denied access to rebuttal documents
Gave the merchant months to respond
Gave the consumer less than 10 days to respond via a letter never received


The denial was not based on evidence of delivery, but on procedural technicalities and an unenforceable merchant narrative.

II.I. Correction Is the Only Compliant Outcome
Maintaining denial under these circumstances creates escalating regulatory exposure. Administrative correction is the only resolution consistent with regulatory expectations and risk management principles. Comparable issuer-side correction has been granted in prior CFPB-supervised billing error matters where provisional credit was ultimately made permanent following issuer escalation.

III. ISSUER LIABILITY, PENALTIES, AND LITIGATION EXPOSURE
If First Interstate Bank continues to enforce an unenforceable merchant narrative after notice, exposure arises under multiple independent authorities:

III.A. Fair Credit Billing Act / TILA (15 U.S.C. §§1666, 1640)
Statutory damages as authorized under civil liability provisions
Actual damages (interest, fees, credit harm, consequential loss)
Mandatory attorney’s fees and costs

III.B. Regulation Z (12 C.F.R. §1026.13)
Civil liability for failure to investigate
Liability for defective notice and explanation
Liability for unlawful collection activity during dispute



III.C. CFPB UDAAP Authority (12 U.S.C. §§5531–5536)
Civil money penalties:
Up to $5,000/day (standard violations)
Up to $25,000/day (reckless violations)
Up to $1,000,000/day (knowing violations)


Restitution orders
Corrective action mandates
Supervisory findings and compliance downgrades



III.D. FTC Act §5 (15 U.S.C. §45)
Liability for facilitating or perpetuating unfair or deceptive practices once on notice


Issuer responsibility where enforcement enables merchant deception


Conclusion: Based on the documented timeline and repeated notice, First Interstate Bank’s exposure substantially exceeds the amount in dispute. Correction materially reduces regulatory, litigation, and reputational risk.

IV. MASTER LEGAL & COMPLIANCE CHECKLIST

IV.0. Burden of Proof Rule
The burden of proof rests with the merchant and issuer, not the consumer. Unsupported allegations require proof of a valid, enforceable agreement.

IV.0.1. No Signed Agreement = No Further Rebuttal Required
Absent production of a signed agreement expressly authorizing alternative pricing or deliverables, no further rebuttal is legally required from the consumer.
A. Transaction & Scope of Agreement
(Foundational - unchanged content preserved)
B. Performance (Objective, Binary Facts)
C. Merchant’s Post-Failure Narrative (Alleged, Not Proven)
D. Contract Formation & Enforceability
E. Fraudulent Inducement & Misrepresentation
F. Retroactive Billing / Changed Terms
G. Bank’s Investigation Failures
H. Defective Notice & Unreasonable Response Window
I. Willful Misconduct / Negligence / Reckless Disregard
J. Missing Material Disclosures (Bank Must Produce)
J-1. Required Issuer Escalation Actions
(All checklist items preserved verbatim)

V. REMEDY & REQUESTED CURE

V.A. Required Billing Correction
Reverse remaining $10,500


Remove all interest and fees


Restore account to pre-dispute status


Provide written confirmation


Close CFPB complaints as resolved


V.B. Compliance Timeline
Administrative correction within 10 business days will fully resolve this matter without further regulatory or judicial escalation.

VI. PRESERVATION / LITIGATION HOLD NOTICE
First Interstate Bank is directed to preserve all records related to this dispute, including internal case notes, escalation logs, call recordings, merchant communications, mailing records, and decision rationales. Failure to preserve will be treated as spoliation.

VII. FINAL NOTE
Nothing above relies on emotion, opinion, or speculation.
 Every claim is either:
A verifiable fact


A documented assertion


Or a demand for proof the merchant or bank must produce


This structure maximizes legal clarity, minimizes exposure for the bank, and supports administrative correction without litigation.

## I. Statutory Basis & Issuer Exposure (Read First)

This matter arises from First Interstate Bank’s mishandling of a federally protected billing error dispute, in violation of the Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666), Regulation Z (12 C.F.R. §1026.13), and federal UDAAP standards enforced by the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau.

Once First Interstate Bank received notice of a billing error, the bank assumed independent, non-delegable statutory duties to:  
• Conduct a reasonable investigation (§1666(a); Reg Z §1026.13(c))  
• Refrain from collection or adverse action during the dispute (§1666(a)(3)(B)(ii))  
• Provide a written explanation of any denial addressing the consumer’s evidence (§1666(a)(3)(B); Reg Z §1026.13(f)) and provide supporting documentation upon request.  
• Avoid reliance on merchant assertions or unenforceable policies  

Failure to meet these duties exposes the issuer to statutory damages, actual damages, attorney’s fees, regulatory enforcement, supervisory findings, and civil money penalties. These obligations apply regardless of merchant refund policies, alleged service hours, or internal merchant narratives.

---

## II. Plain-English Executive Summary

PART I - PLAIN-ENGLISH EXECUTIVE SUMMARY  
(this frames everything that follows)  
This dispute is not just “services not rendered.”  
 It is a pattern of misrepresentation, lack of agreement, retroactive billing, and procedural unfairness, compounded by the bank’s failure to conduct a reasonable investigation. There is a pattern of conduct that, once notice was provided, supports findings of willful non-compliance, willful negligence, willful misconduct, and reckless disregard.  

What actually happened - in simple terms:  
I purchased a Done-For-You (DFY) Airbnb service, NOT consulting.  
The merchant promised (verified in already provided documentation: ads, website, sales calls, written materials, graphics, and transcripts) to:  

Acquire a property  
Furnish it  
List it  
Deliver a cash-flowing Airbnb generating ~$3,500-$5,000+/month  
With explicit representations such as: “If we don’t acquire you a property, you pay $0.”  
Explicit week by week timeline of deliverables  

None of that happened. (all proven and verified factually and indisputably via already provided ads, website, sales calls, written materials, graphics, and transcripts)  

No property acquired  
No lease signed  
No furnishing  
No listing  
No revenue  

After failing to deliver, the merchant changed the story:  
They retroactively claimed the money was for “consulting”  
They alleged 109 hours at $45/hour to justify retaining $10,500 (unsupported merchant allegation; unproven, undocumented, and never consented to) - notwithstanding that the consumer’s participation consisted of occasional, optionally scheduled weekly calls of approximately one hour.  

The asserted “109 hours at $45/hour” figure is not a term agreed to by the consumer, nor is it supported by any executed agreement, disclosure, or consent. This figure is an unsupported allegation made by the merchant and relayed by the bank, introduced only after the merchant failed to deliver the promised Done-For-You Airbnb service. At no point did the consumer agree to hourly consulting, time-based billing, or any pricing structure contingent on hours worked rather than outcome delivered. The consumer’s actual participation consisted of occasional weekly update calls of approximately one hour, which were ancillary to the promised DFY service and never represented or disclosed as billable consulting.  

No contemporaneous time records, invoices, or consumer-acknowledged logs have been produced.  

This hourly billing:  

Was never disclosed  
Was never agreed to  
Was never signed  
Was never part of the deal  

In reality, I only had occasional weekly calls (~1 hour each), which were calls to keep me up to date, not paid hourly consulting.  

Under FTC Act §5, the transaction is defined by the net impression of the merchant’s advertising and sales representations.  
All representations in this matter concerned a Done-For-You Airbnb acquisition and handover, not hourly consulting.  
The merchant’s post-hoc claim of ‘109 hours at $45/hour’ was never disclosed, never agreed to, and is materially inconsistent with the advertised deliverables, including express representations that no payment would be owed absent property acquisition. (unsupported merchant allegation; no signed agreement, disclosure, or consumer consent exists)  

The FTC treats such retroactive recharacterization as deceptive, unreasonable, and unenforceable. Reliance on unsigned or undisclosed terms cannot cure or override deceptive advertising.  

These FTC Act §5 standards are directly applicable to First Interstate Bank as the issuer responsible for conducting a reasonable investigation and for avoiding unfair or deceptive practices in dispute resolution.  
Once the bank is placed on notice that a merchant’s post-transaction narrative is materially inconsistent with the net impression of the advertising and sales representations, the bank may not lawfully rely on that narrative to deny a billing error claim.  
Accepting and enforcing an undisclosed, unsigned, and retroactively imposed billing theory, particularly one that contradicts express outcome-based promises; exposes the bank to liability for facilitating or perpetuating an unfair practice, failing to conduct a reasonable investigation, and improperly shifting the burden of the proof to the consumer.  
Federal regulators, including the CFPB, evaluate such conduct as an independent compliance failure, regardless of merchant misconduct, where the issuer disregards clear evidence of deceptive inducement, lack of assent, and non-delivery.  
Continued enforcement after notice materially increases regulatory exposure, corrective action risk, restitution obligations, and supervisory findings.  

Once First Interstate Bank was placed on actual notice of the material misrepresentations, lack of mutual assent, unenforceable and unsigned terms, and total non-delivery of the promised Done-For-You Airbnb service, the bank assumed an independent legal obligation to correct the billing error and conduct a reasonable, evidence-based investigation. At that point, continued reliance on the merchant’s post-hoc narrative, particularly one that contradicts the net impression of the merchant’s own advertising and sales representations - constitutes a separate and independent compliance failure by the issuer.  
Issuer obligations under the FCBA and Regulation Z are non-delegable and cannot be satisfied by deferring to merchant assertions where the evidentiary record contradicts those assertions.  

Federal regulators do not permit issuers to defer to merchant assertions where the record demonstrates deceptive inducement, non-delivery, and lack of consumer consent. Continued enforcement after notice exposes the bank to issuer-side liability under Regulation Z, UDAAP standards enforced by the CFPB, and unfair-practice facilitation principles recognized under the FTC Act, regardless of whether the merchant itself is separately pursued.  

There is no enforceable agreement supporting the merchant’s claims:  
No signed contract  
No DocuSign  
No click-wrap  
No assent  
No meeting of the minds  
No agreement authorizing hourly billing  
No agreement allowing changed terms  
No agreement making the transaction non-refundable  

Because no executed agreement exists authorizing hourly consulting, retroactive billing, modified deliverables, or non-refundability, the merchant’s asserted “109 hours at $45/hour” theory is a legal nullity. Under basic contract law principles, a party cannot impose material pricing terms, scope changes, or billing structures absent mutual assent. An unsigned, undisclosed, or unaccepted document - whether characterized as “terms,” “policies,” or “internal agreements” - has no legal force and cannot be enforced by the merchant or adopted by the issuer. Any attempt to retain funds on this basis constitutes retention without contractual or legal authority.  

Despite this, the bank:  
Enforced unsigned terms  
Shifted the burden of proof to the consumer  
Accepted merchant allegations without proof  
Denied access to rebuttal documents  
Gave the merchant months to respond  
Gave the consumer less than 10 days to respond - via a letter never received  

The denial was not based on evidence of delivery.  
 It was based on procedural technicalities and an unenforceable merchant narrative.  

Maintaining denial under these circumstances, where the issuer has been provided overwhelming, documented evidence of deceptive inducement, non-delivery, lack of assent, and unenforceable terms - creates escalating regulatory exposure for First Interstate Bank.  
Under CFPB enforcement authority, issuers that engage in or perpetuate unfair, deceptive, or unreasonable dispute-resolution practices may be subject to restitution orders, corrective action mandates, supervisory findings, and civil money penalties.  
CFPB civil penalty tiers currently authorize fines of up to $5,000 per day for standard violations, up to $25,000 per day for reckless violations, and up to $1,000,000 per day for knowing violations, with exposure increasing once an institution has actual notice and continues the challenged conduct.  
Administrative correction at this stage is the only resolution consistent with regulatory expectations and risk management principles.  

Comparable issuer-side correction after escalation has been granted in prior CFPB-supervised billing error matters where provisional credit was ultimately made permanent following issuer escalation.
