## III. Issuer Liability, Penalties, and Litigation Exposure

Issuer Liability, Penalties, and Litigation Exposure  
If First Interstate Bank continues to enforce an unenforceable merchant narrative after notice, the bank faces escalating exposure under multiple independent authorities, including but not limited to:  

1. Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666)  
 • Statutory damages as authorized under the Truth in Lending Act and Fair Credit Billing Act civil liability provisions (15 U.S.C. §1640), together with:  
 • Actual damages (interest, fees, credit harm, consequential loss)  
	 • Mandatory attorney’s fees and costs  

2. Regulation Z (12 C.F.R. §1026.13)  
 • Civil liability for failure to investigate  
 • Liability for defective notice and explanation  
 • Liability for unlawful collection activity during dispute  

3. CFPB UDAAP Authority (12 U.S.C. §§5531–5536)  
 Civil money penalties of:  
 - Up to $5,000 per day (standard violations)  
 - Up to $25,000 per day (reckless violations)  
 -  Up to $1,000,000 per day (knowing violations)  
 - Restitution orders  
 - Corrective action mandates  
 - Supervisory findings and compliance downgrades  

4. FTC Act §5 (15 U.S.C. §45)  
 • Liability for facilitating or perpetuating unfair or deceptive practices once on notice  
 • Issuer responsibility where enforcement enables merchant deception  

Based on the documented timeline, repeated notice, and continued enforcement, First Interstate Bank’s exposure substantially exceeds the amount in dispute. Correction at this stage materially reduces regulatory, litigation, and reputational risk.

This is why this matter involves:  
Fraudulent inducement  
Material misrepresentation  
Lack of mutual assent  
Retroactive billing  
Unfair and unreasonable investigation practices  
Willful or reckless disregard once notice was provided  
Willful non-compliance  
Willful misconduct  

The checklist below breaks this down line-by-line, provable fact-by-fact.

2. Regulation Z (12 C.F.R. §1026.13)
 • Civil liability for failure to investigate
 • Liability for defective notice and explanation
 • Liability for unlawful collection activity during dispute

3. CFPB UDAAP Authority (12 U.S.C. §§5531–5536)
 Civil money penalties of:
 - Up to $5,000 per day (standard violations)

 - Restitution orders
 - Corrective action mandates
 - Supervisory findings and compliance downgrades

4. FTC Act §5 (15 U.S.C. §45)
 • Liability for facilitating or perpetuating unfair or deceptive practices once on notice
 • Issuer responsibility where enforcement enables merchant deception
Based on the documented timeline, repeated notice, and continued enforcement, First Interstate Bank’s exposure substantially exceeds the amount in dispute. Correction at this stage materially reduces regulatory, litigation, and reputational risk.

I. Statutory Basis & Issuer Exposure 
(Read First)

This matter arises from First Interstate Bank’s mishandling of a federally protected billing error dispute, in violation of the Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666), Regulation Z (12 C.F.R. §1026.13), and federal UDAAP standards enforced by the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau.
I.A. Trigger Event - Notice of Billing Error
 • Once First Interstate Bank received notice of a billing error, the bank assumed independent, non-delegable statutory duties.
I.B. Duty 1 - Reasonable Investigation
 • Conduct a reasonable investigation (§1666(a); Reg Z §1026.13(c))
I.C. Duty 2 - No Collection / Adverse Action During Dispute
 • Refrain from collection or adverse action during the dispute (§1666(a)(3)(B)(ii))
I.D. Duty 3 - Written Explanation + Supporting Documentation
 • Provide a written explanation of any denial addressing the consumer’s evidence (§1666(a)(3)(B); Reg Z §1026.13(f)) and provide supporting documentation upon request.
I.E. Duty 4 - No Reliance on Merchant Assertions / Unenforceable Policies
 • Avoid reliance on merchant assertions or unenforceable policies

Failure to meet these duties exposes the issuer to statutory damages, actual damages, attorney’s fees, regulatory enforcement, supervisory findings, and civil money penalties. These obligations apply regardless of merchant refund policies, alleged service hours, or internal merchant narratives.


II. PLAIN-ENGLISH EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

(this frames everything that follows)

This dispute is not just “services not rendered.”

It is a pattern of misrepresentation, lack of agreement, retroactive billing, and procedural unfairness, compounded by the bank’s failure to conduct a reasonable investigation. There is a pattern of conduct that, once notice was provided, supports findings of willful non-compliance, willful negligence, willful misconduct, and reckless disregard.



II.1 What Was Purchased & Promised

I purchased a Done-For-You (DFY) Airbnb service, NOT consulting.
The merchant promised (verified in already provided documentation: ads, website, sales calls, written materials, graphics, and transcripts) to:


Acquire a property
Furnish it
List it
Deliver a cash-flowing Airbnb generating ~$3,500-$5,000+/month
With explicit representations such as: “If we don’t acquire you a property, you pay $0.”
Explicit week by week timeline of deliverables 


None of that happened. (all proven and verified factually and indisputably via already provided ads, website, sales calls, written materials, graphics, and transcripts)


No property acquired
No lease signed
No furnishing
No listing
No revenue




II.2 Post-Failure Recharacterization - Non-Delivery Downscale, Partial Refund, and Unenforceable Retroactive Billing (109 Hours / $45 per Hour)
Merchant confirms missed timelines and non deliveries, and allows the client to downscale from 2 delivered properties, down to 1 property with the purpose of full delivery, for a partial refund of $7,500. (Confirmed in provided proven documentation via text messages and emails)

Once the merchant fails to deliver even 1 property within late, extended timelines; the client pursues legal chargeback remedies via fraudulent inducement, misrepresentation, and non delivery.

In response to the chargeback claim, the merchant responds that:
They retroactively claimed the money was for “consulting”
They alleged 109 hours at $45/hour to justify retaining $10,500 (unsupported merchant allegation; unproven, undocumented, and never consented to) - notwithstanding that the consumer’s participation consisted of occasional, optionally scheduled weekly calls of approximately one hour.
The asserted “109 hours at $45/hour” figure is not a term agreed to by the consumer, nor is it supported by any executed agreement, disclosure, or consent. This figure is an unsupported allegation made by the merchant and relayed by the bank, introduced only after the merchant failed to deliver the promised Done-For-You Airbnb service. At no point did the consumer agree to hourly consulting, time-based billing, or any pricing structure contingent on hours worked rather than outcome delivered. The consumer’s actual participation consisted of occasional weekly update calls of approximately one hour, which were ancillary to the promised DFY service and never represented or disclosed as billable consulting.

No contemporaneous time records, invoices, or consumer-acknowledged logs have been produced.

This hourly billing:
Was never disclosed
Was never agreed to
Was never signed
Was never part of the deal


In reality, I only had occasional weekly calls (~1 hour each), which were calls to keep me up to date, not paid hourly consulting.



II.3 Net Impression + Issuer UDAAP Exposure

Under FTC Act §5, the transaction is defined by the net impression of the merchant’s advertising and sales representations. 

All representations in this matter concerned a Done-For-You Airbnb acquisition and handover, not hourly consulting. 

The merchant’s post-hoc claim of ‘109 hours at $45/hour’ was never disclosed, never agreed to, and is materially inconsistent with the advertised deliverables, including express representations that no payment would be owed absent property acquisition. (unsupported merchant allegation; no signed agreement, disclosure, or consumer consent exists)

The FTC treats such retroactive recharacterization as deceptive, unreasonable, and unenforceable. Reliance on unsigned or undisclosed terms cannot cure or override deceptive advertising.

These FTC Act §5 standards are directly applicable to First Interstate Bank as the issuer responsible for conducting a reasonable investigation and for avoiding unfair or deceptive practices in dispute resolution. 

Once the bank is placed on notice that a merchant’s post-transaction narrative is materially inconsistent with the net impression of the advertising and sales representations, the bank may not lawfully rely on that narrative to deny a billing error claim. 

II.3A) Compliance

Accepting and enforcing an undisclosed, unsigned, and retroactively imposed billing theory, particularly one that contradicts express outcome-based promises; exposes the bank to liability for facilitating or perpetuating an unfair practice, failing to conduct a reasonable investigation, and improperly shifting the burden of proof to the consumer. 

Federal regulators, including the CFPB, evaluate such conduct as an independent compliance failure, regardless of merchant misconduct, where the issuer disregards clear evidence of deceptive inducement, lack of assent, and non-delivery. 
Continued enforcement after notice materially increases regulatory exposure, corrective action risk, restitution obligations, and supervisory findings.


II.3B) Notice

Once First Interstate Bank was placed on actual notice of the material misrepresentations, lack of mutual assent, unenforceable and unsigned terms, and total non-delivery of the promised Done-For-You Airbnb service, the bank assumed an independent legal obligation to correct the billing error and conduct a reasonable, evidence-based investigation. At that point, continued reliance on the merchant’s post-hoc narrative, particularly one that contradicts the net impression of the merchant’s own advertising and sales representations - constitutes a separate and independent compliance failure by the issuer. 

Issuer obligations under the FCBA and Regulation Z are non-delegable and cannot be satisfied by deferring to merchant assertions where the evidentiary record contradicts those assertions.

Federal regulators do not permit issuers to defer to merchant assertions where the record demonstrates deceptive inducement, non-delivery, and lack of consumer consent. Continued enforcement after notice exposes the bank to issuer-side liability under Regulation Z, UDAAP standards enforced by the CFPB, and unfair-practice facilitation principles recognized under the FTC Act, regardless of whether the merchant itself is separately pursued.



II.4 No Contract / No Assent / No Enforceable Terms

There is no enforceable agreement supporting the merchant’s claims:

No signed contract
No DocuSign
No click-wrap
No assent
No meeting of the minds
No agreement authorizing hourly billing
No agreement allowing changed terms
No agreement making the transaction non-refundable


Because no executed agreement exists authorizing hourly consulting, retroactive billing, modified deliverables, or non-refundability, the merchant’s asserted “109 hours at $45/hour” theory is a legal nullity. Under basic contract law principles, a party cannot impose material pricing terms, scope changes, or billing structures absent mutual assent. An unsigned, undisclosed, or unaccepted document - whether characterized as “terms,” “policies,” or “internal agreements” - has no legal force and cannot be enforced by the merchant or adopted by the issuer. Any attempt to retain funds on this basis constitutes retention without contractual or legal authority.



Required proof to sustain denial (must be produced): (a) signed governing agreement; (b) signed hourly billing authorization; (c) itemized time records acknowledged by consumer; (d) proof of disclosure pre-payment; (e) proof of delivery of DFY outcomes; (f) full representment packet and case log.

Despite this, the bank:
Enforced unsigned terms
Shifted the burden of proof to the consumer
Accepted merchant allegations without proof
Denied access to rebuttal documents
Gave the merchant months to respond
Gave the consumer less than 10 days to respond - via a letter never received





II.5 Denial Basis = Procedure, Not Delivery

The denial was not based on evidence of delivery.


It was based on procedural technicalities and an unenforceable merchant narrative.
Maintaining denial under these circumstances, where the issuer has been provided overwhelming, documented evidence of deceptive inducement, non-delivery, lack of assent, and unenforceable terms - creates escalating regulatory exposure for First Interstate Bank. 

Under CFPB enforcement authority, issuers that engage in or perpetuate unfair, deceptive, or unreasonable dispute-resolution practices are subject to restitution orders, corrective action mandates, supervisory findings, and civil money penalties. 

CFPB civil penalty tiers currently authorize fines of up to $5,000 per day for standard violations, up to $25,000 per day for reckless violations, and up to $1,000,000 per day for knowing violations, with exposure increasing once an institution has actual notice and continues the challenged conduct. 

Administrative correction at this stage is the only resolution consistent with regulatory expectations and risk management principles.

Comparable issuer-side correction after escalation has been granted in prior CFPB-supervised billing error matters where provisional credit was ultimately made permanent following issuer escalation.


III. Issuer Liability, Penalties, and Litigation Exposure


If First Interstate Bank continues to enforce an unenforceable merchant narrative after notice, the bank faces escalating exposure under multiple independent authorities, including but not limited to:

1. Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666)
 • Statutory damages as authorized under the Truth in Lending Act and Fair Credit Billing Act civil liability provisions (15 U.S.C. §1640), together with:
 • Actual damages (interest, fees, credit harm, consequential loss)
	 • Mandatory attorney’s fees and costs

2. Regulation Z (12 C.F.R. §1026.13)
 • Civil liability for failure to investigate
 • Liability for defective notice and explanation
 • Liability for unlawful collection activity during dispute

3. CFPB UDAAP Authority (12 U.S.C. §§5531–5536)
 Civil money penalties of:

 - Up to $25,000 per day (reckless violations)

 • Liability for facilitating or perpetuating unfair or deceptive practices once on notice

## 👤 You

PENDING LITIGATION - INTENT TO RESOLVE QUICKLY AND AMICABLY

Please escalate this matter to the highest executive resolution authority with power to reverse/restore credit and direct card-network escalation. 

Please ALSO forward to Legal/Compliance for formal review of FCBA/Reg Z duties, investigation adequacy, and documentation obligations.



I. Statutory Basis & Issuer Exposure (Read First)
II. Plain-English Executive Summary
III. Issuer Liability, Penalties, and Litigation Exposure
IV. Master Legal & Compliance Checklist
V. Remedy & Requested Cure
VI. Preservation / Litigation Hold
VII. Final Note


I. Statutory Basis & Issuer Exposure 
(Read First)

This matter arises from First Interstate Bank’s mishandling of a federally protected billing error dispute, in violation of the Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666), Regulation Z (12 C.F.R. §1026.13), and federal UDAAP standards enforced by the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau.
I.A. Trigger Event - Notice of Billing Error
 • Once First Interstate Bank received notice of a billing error, the bank assumed independent, non-delegable statutory duties.
I.B. Duty 1 - Reasonable Investigation
 • Conduct a reasonable investigation (§1666(a); Reg Z §1026.13(c))
I.C. Duty 2 - No Collection / Adverse Action During Dispute
 • Refrain from collection or adverse action during the dispute (§1666(a)(3)(B)(ii))
I.D. Duty 3 - Written Explanation + Supporting Documentation
 • Provide a written explanation of any denial addressing the consumer’s evidence (§1666(a)(3)(B); Reg Z §1026.13(f)) and provide supporting documentation upon request.
I.E. Duty 4 - No Reliance on Merchant Assertions / Unenforceable Policies
 • Avoid reliance on merchant assertions or unenforceable policies

Failure to meet these duties exposes the issuer to statutory damages, actual damages, attorney’s fees, regulatory enforcement, supervisory findings, and civil money penalties. These obligations apply regardless of merchant refund policies, alleged service hours, or internal merchant narratives.


II. PLAIN-ENGLISH EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

(this frames everything that follows)

This dispute is not just “services not rendered.”

It is a pattern of misrepresentation, lack of agreement, retroactive billing, and procedural unfairness, compounded by the bank’s failure to conduct a reasonable investigation. There is a pattern of conduct that, once notice was provided, supports findings of willful non-compliance, willful negligence, willful misconduct, and reckless disregard.



II.1 What Was Purchased & Promised

I purchased a Done-For-You (DFY) Airbnb service, NOT consulting.
The merchant promised (verified in already provided documentation: ads, website, sales calls, written materials, graphics, and transcripts) to:


Acquire a property
Furnish it
List it
Deliver a cash-flowing Airbnb generating ~$3,500-$5,000+/month
With explicit representations such as: “If we don’t acquire you a property, you pay $0.”
Explicit week by week timeline of deliverables 


None of that happened. (all proven and verified factually and indisputably via already provided ads, website, sales calls, written materials, graphics, and transcripts)


No property acquired
No lease signed
No furnishing
No listing
No revenue




II.2 Post-Failure Recharacterization - Non-Delivery Downscale, Partial Refund, and Unenforceable Retroactive Billing (109 Hours / $45 per Hour)

Documented & Provided Record confirms non-delivery and downscale/partial refund: The merchant acknowledged missed timelines/non-delivery and agreed to a downscale (from two properties to one) and issued a partial refund of $7,500 (as documented in the provided texts/emails).

After continued non-delivery, a billing error dispute was initiated: After the merchant still failed to deliver the promised DFY (Done-For-You) outcome, the consumer initiated billing-error/chargeback remedies based on misrepresentation, lack of assent, and non-delivery. (Documentation already provided multiple times)
This keeps it “black-and-white record” instead of “argument.”

Once the merchant fails again to deliver even 1 promised property within grace-granted late extended timelines in good-faith; the client pursues legal chargeback remedies via fraudulent inducement, misrepresentation, and non delivery.

In response to the chargeback claim, the merchant responds that:
They retroactively claimed the money was for “consulting”
They alleged 109 hours at $45/hour to justify retaining $10,500 (unsupported merchant allegation; unproven, undocumented, and never consented to) - notwithstanding that the consumer’s participation consisted of occasional, optionally scheduled weekly calls of approximately one hour.
The asserted “109 hours at $45/hour” figure is not a term agreed to by the consumer, nor is it supported by any executed agreement, disclosure, or consent. This figure is an unsupported allegation made by the merchant and relayed by the bank, introduced only after the merchant failed to deliver the promised Done-For-You Airbnb service. At no point did the consumer agree to hourly consulting, time-based billing, or any pricing structure contingent on hours worked rather than outcome delivered. The consumer’s actual participation consisted of occasional weekly update calls of approximately one hour, which were ancillary to the promised DFY service and never represented or disclosed as billable consulting.

No contemporaneous time records, invoices, or consumer-acknowledged logs have been produced.

This hourly billing:
Was never disclosed
Was never agreed to
Was never signed
Was never part of the deal


In reality, I only had occasional weekly calls (~1 hour each), which were calls to keep me up to date, not paid hourly consulting.



II.3 Net Impression + Issuer UDAAP Exposure

Under FTC Act §5, the transaction is defined by the net impression of the merchant’s advertising and sales representations. 

All representations in this matter concerned a Done-For-You Airbnb acquisition and handover, not hourly consulting. 

The merchant’s post-hoc claim of ‘109 hours at $45/hour’ was never disclosed, never agreed to, and is materially inconsistent with the advertised deliverables, including express representations that no payment would be owed absent property acquisition. (unsupported merchant allegation; no signed agreement, disclosure, or consumer consent exists)

The FTC treats such retroactive recharacterization as deceptive, unreasonable, and unenforceable. Reliance on unsigned or undisclosed terms cannot cure or override deceptive advertising.

These FTC Act §5 standards are directly applicable to First Interstate Bank as the issuer responsible for conducting a reasonable investigation and for avoiding unfair or deceptive practices in dispute resolution. 

Once the bank is placed on notice that a merchant’s post-transaction narrative is materially inconsistent with the net impression of the advertising and sales representations, the bank may not lawfully rely on that narrative to deny a billing error claim. 

II.3A) Compliance

Accepting and enforcing an undisclosed, unsigned, and retroactively imposed billing theory, particularly one that contradicts express outcome-based promises; exposes the bank to liability for facilitating or perpetuating an unfair practice, failing to conduct a reasonable investigation, and improperly shifting the burden of proof to the consumer. 

Federal regulators, including the CFPB, evaluate such conduct as an independent compliance failure, regardless of merchant misconduct, where the issuer disregards clear evidence of deceptive inducement, lack of assent, and non-delivery. 
Continued enforcement after notice materially increases regulatory exposure, corrective action risk, restitution obligations, and supervisory findings.


II.3B) Notice

Once First Interstate Bank was placed on actual notice of the material misrepresentations, lack of mutual assent, unenforceable and unsigned terms, and total non-delivery of the promised Done-For-You Airbnb service, the bank assumed an independent legal obligation to correct the billing error and conduct a reasonable, evidence-based investigation. At that point, continued reliance on the merchant’s post-hoc narrative, particularly one that contradicts the net impression of the merchant’s own advertising and sales representations - constitutes a separate and independent compliance failure by the issuer. 

Issuer obligations under the FCBA and Regulation Z are non-delegable and cannot be satisfied by deferring to merchant assertions where the evidentiary record contradicts those assertions.

Federal regulators do not permit issuers to defer to merchant assertions where the record demonstrates deceptive inducement, non-delivery, and lack of consumer consent. Continued enforcement after notice exposes the bank to issuer-side liability under Regulation Z, UDAAP standards enforced by the CFPB, and unfair-practice facilitation principles recognized under the FTC Act, regardless of whether the merchant itself is separately pursued.

Issuer must prove (or obtain from the merchant) evidence of (i) agreed terms and (ii) actual delivery; absent that, denial is noncompliant and cannot rest on post-hoc assertions.



II.4 No Contract / No Assent / No Enforceable Terms

There is no enforceable agreement supporting the merchant’s claims:

No signed contract
No DocuSign
No click-wrap
No assent
No meeting of the minds
No agreement authorizing hourly billing
No agreement allowing changed terms
No agreement making the transaction non-refundable


Because no executed agreement exists authorizing hourly consulting, retroactive billing, modified deliverables, or non-refundability, the merchant’s asserted “109 hours at $45/hour” theory is a legal nullity. Under basic contract law principles, a party cannot impose material pricing terms, scope changes, or billing structures absent mutual assent. An unsigned, undisclosed, or unaccepted document - whether characterized as “terms,” “policies,” or “internal agreements” - has no legal force and cannot be enforced by the merchant or adopted by the issuer. Any attempt to retain funds on this basis constitutes retention without contractual or legal authority.



II.4A Required Proof to Sustain Denial (Must Be Produced)

Required proof to sustain denial (must be produced): (a) signed governing agreement; (b) signed hourly billing authorization; (c) itemized time records acknowledged by consumer; (d) proof of disclosure pre-payment; (e) proof of delivery of DFY outcomes; (f) full representment packet and case log.

Despite this, the bank:
Enforced unsigned terms
Shifted the burden of proof to the consumer
Accepted merchant allegations without proof
Denied access to rebuttal documents
Gave the merchant months to respond
Gave the consumer less than 10 days to respond - via a letter never received





II.5 Denial Basis = Procedure, Not Delivery

The denial was not based on evidence of delivery.


It was based on procedural technicalities and an unenforceable merchant narrative.
Maintaining denial under these circumstances, where the issuer has been provided overwhelming, documented evidence of deceptive inducement, non-delivery, lack of assent, and unenforceable terms - creates escalating regulatory exposure for First Interstate Bank. 

Under CFPB enforcement authority, issuers that engage in or perpetuate unfair, deceptive, or unreasonable dispute-resolution practices are subject to restitution orders, corrective action mandates, supervisory findings, and civil money penalties. 

CFPB civil penalty tiers currently authorize fines of up to $5,000 per day for standard violations, up to $25,000 per day for reckless violations, and up to $1,000,000 per day for knowing violations, with exposure increasing once an institution has actual notice and continues the challenged conduct. 

Administrative correction at this stage is the only resolution consistent with regulatory expectations and risk management principles.

Comparable issuer-side correction after escalation has been granted in prior CFPB-supervised billing error matters where provisional credit was ultimately made permanent following issuer escalation.


III. Issuer Liability, Penalties, and Litigation Exposure


If First Interstate Bank continues to enforce an unenforceable merchant narrative after notice, the bank faces escalating exposure under multiple independent authorities, including but not limited to:

1. Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666)
 • Statutory damages as authorized under the Truth in Lending Act and Fair Credit Billing Act civil liability provisions (15 U.S.C. §1640), together with:
 • Actual damages (interest, fees, credit harm, consequential loss)
	 • Mandatory attorney’s fees and costs

2. Regulation Z (12 C.F.R. §1026.13)
 • Civil liability for failure to investigate
 • Liability for defective notice and explanation
 • Liability for unlawful collection activity during dispute

3. CFPB UDAAP Authority (12 U.S.C. §§5531–5536)
 Civil money penalties of:
 - Up to $5,000 per day (standard violations)
 - Up to $25,000 per day (reckless violations)
 -  Up to $1,000,000 per day (knowing violations)
 - Restitution orders
 - Corrective action mandates
 - Supervisory findings and compliance downgrades

4. FTC Act §5 (15 U.S.C. §45)
 • Liability for facilitating or perpetuating unfair or deceptive practices once on notice
 • Issuer responsibility where enforcement enables merchant deception
Based on the documented timeline, repeated notice, and continued enforcement, First Interstate Bank’s exposure substantially exceeds the amount in dispute. Correction at this stage materially reduces regulatory, litigation, and reputational risk.

PENDING LITIGATION - INTENT TO RESOLVE QUICKLY AND AMICABLY

Please escalate this matter to the highest executive resolution authority with power to reverse/restore credit and direct card-network escalation. 

Please ALSO forward to Legal/Compliance for formal review of FCBA/Reg Z duties, investigation adequacy, and documentation obligations.



I. Statutory Basis & Issuer Exposure (Read First)
II. Plain-English Executive Summary
III. Issuer Liability, Penalties, and Litigation Exposure
IV. Master Legal & Compliance Checklist
V. Remedy & Requested Cure
VI. Preservation / Litigation Hold
VII. Final Note


I. Statutory Basis & Issuer Exposure 
(Read First)

This matter arises from First Interstate Bank’s mishandling of a federally protected billing error dispute, in violation of the Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666), Regulation Z (12 C.F.R. §1026.13), and federal UDAAP standards enforced by the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau.
I.A. Trigger Event - Notice of Billing Error
 • Once First Interstate Bank received notice of a billing error, the bank assumed independent, non-delegable statutory duties.
I.B. Duty 1 - Reasonable Investigation
 • Conduct a reasonable investigation (§1666(a); Reg Z §1026.13(c))
I.C. Duty 2 - No Collection / Adverse Action During Dispute
 • Refrain from collection or adverse action during the dispute (§1666(a)(3)(B)(ii))
I.D. Duty 3 - Written Explanation + Supporting Documentation
 • Provide a written explanation of any denial addressing the consumer’s evidence (§1666(a)(3)(B); Reg Z §1026.13(f)) and provide supporting documentation upon request.
I.E. Duty 4 - No Reliance on Merchant Assertions / Unenforceable Policies
 • Avoid reliance on merchant assertions or unenforceable policies

Failure to meet these duties exposes the issuer to statutory damages, actual damages, attorney’s fees, regulatory enforcement, supervisory findings, and civil money penalties. These obligations apply regardless of merchant refund policies, alleged service hours, or internal merchant narratives.


II. PLAIN-ENGLISH EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

(this frames everything that follows)

This dispute is not just “services not rendered.”

It is a pattern of misrepresentation, lack of agreement, retroactive billing, and procedural unfairness, compounded by the bank’s failure to conduct a reasonable investigation. There is a pattern of conduct that, once notice was provided, supports findings of willful non-compliance, willful negligence, willful misconduct, and reckless disregard.



II.1 What Was Purchased & Promised

I purchased a Done-For-You (DFY) Airbnb service, NOT consulting.
The merchant promised (verified in already provided documentation: ads, website, sales calls, written materials, graphics, and transcripts) to:


Acquire a property
Furnish it
List it
Deliver a cash-flowing Airbnb generating ~$3,500-$5,000+/month
With explicit representations such as: “If we don’t acquire you a property, you pay $0.”
Explicit week by week timeline of deliverables 


None of that happened. (all proven and verified factually and indisputably via already provided ads, website, sales calls, written materials, graphics, and transcripts)


No property acquired
No lease signed
No furnishing
No listing
No revenue




II.2 Post-Failure Recharacterization - Non-Delivery Downscale, Partial Refund, and Unenforceable Retroactive Billing (109 Hours / $45 per Hour)

Documented & Provided Record confirms non-delivery and downscale/partial refund: The merchant acknowledged missed timelines/non-delivery and agreed to a downscale (from two properties to one) and issued a partial refund of $7,500 (as documented in the provided texts/emails).

After continued non-delivery, a billing error dispute was initiated: After the merchant still failed to deliver the promised DFY (Done-For-You) outcome, the consumer initiated billing-error/chargeback remedies based on misrepresentation, lack of assent, and non-delivery. (Documentation already provided multiple times)
Following the partial refund and downscale, the consumer granted the merchant an additional extension and opportunity to deliver the remaining single promised property. Despite this extension and additional time beyond the original and revised timelines, the merchant failed to acquire, furnish, list, or deliver even one revenue-producing Airbnb property as promised, marketed, advertised, and transcribed. This extended non-delivery is documented in the record.

In response to the chargeback claim, following their second opportunity for failed delivery of promised and fraudulently induced misrepresented deliverables of a DFY Airbnb profiting , furnished, & listed property, the merchant responds that:
They retroactively claimed the money was for “consulting”
They alleged 109 hours at $45/hour to justify retaining $10,500 (unsupported merchant allegation; unproven, undocumented, and never consented to) - notwithstanding that the consumer’s participation consisted of occasional, optionally scheduled weekly calls of approximately one hour.
The asserted “109 hours at $45/hour” figure is not a term agreed to by the consumer, nor is it supported by any executed agreement, disclosure, or consent. This figure is an unsupported allegation made by the merchant and relayed by the bank, introduced only after the merchant failed to deliver the promised Done-For-You Airbnb service. At no point did the consumer agree to hourly consulting, time-based billing, or any pricing structure contingent on hours worked rather than outcome delivered. The consumer’s actual participation consisted of occasional weekly update calls of approximately one hour, which were ancillary to the promised DFY service and never represented or disclosed as billable consulting.

No contemporaneous time records, invoices, or consumer-acknowledged logs have been produced.

This hourly billing:
Was never disclosed
Was never agreed to
Was never signed
Was never part of the deal


In reality, I only had occasional weekly calls (~1 hour each), which were calls to keep me up to date, not paid hourly consulting.



II.3 Net Impression + Issuer UDAAP Exposure

Under FTC Act §5, the transaction is defined by the net impression of the merchant’s advertising and sales representations. 

All representations in this matter concerned a Done-For-You Airbnb acquisition and handover, not hourly consulting. 

The merchant’s post-hoc claim of ‘109 hours at $45/hour’ was never disclosed, never agreed to, and is materially inconsistent with the advertised deliverables, including express representations that no payment would be owed absent property acquisition. (unsupported merchant allegation; no signed agreement, disclosure, or consumer consent exists)

The FTC treats such retroactive recharacterization as deceptive, unreasonable, and unenforceable. Reliance on unsigned or undisclosed terms cannot cure or override deceptive advertising.

These FTC Act §5 standards are directly applicable to First Interstate Bank as the issuer responsible for conducting a reasonable investigation and for avoiding unfair or deceptive practices in dispute resolution. 

Once the bank is placed on notice that a merchant’s post-transaction narrative is materially inconsistent with the net impression of the advertising and sales representations, the bank may not lawfully rely on that narrative to deny a billing error claim. 

II.3A) Compliance

Accepting and enforcing an undisclosed, unsigned, and retroactively imposed billing theory, particularly one that contradicts express outcome-based promises; exposes the bank to liability for facilitating or perpetuating an unfair practice, failing to conduct a reasonable investigation, and improperly shifting the burden of proof to the consumer. 

Federal regulators, including the CFPB, evaluate such conduct as an independent compliance failure, regardless of merchant misconduct, where the issuer disregards clear evidence of deceptive inducement, lack of assent, and non-delivery. 
Continued enforcement after notice materially increases regulatory exposure, corrective action risk, restitution obligations, and supervisory findings.


II.3B) Notice

Once First Interstate Bank was placed on actual notice of the material misrepresentations, lack of mutual assent, unenforceable and unsigned terms, and total non-delivery of the promised Done-For-You Airbnb service, the bank assumed an independent legal obligation to correct the billing error and conduct a reasonable, evidence-based investigation. At that point, continued reliance on the merchant’s post-hoc narrative, particularly one that contradicts the net impression of the merchant’s own advertising and sales representations - constitutes a separate and independent compliance failure by the issuer. 

Issuer obligations under the FCBA and Regulation Z are non-delegable and cannot be satisfied by deferring to merchant assertions where the evidentiary record contradicts those assertions.

Federal regulators do not permit issuers to defer to merchant assertions where the record demonstrates deceptive inducement, non-delivery, and lack of consumer consent. Continued enforcement after notice exposes the bank to issuer-side liability under Regulation Z, UDAAP standards enforced by the CFPB, and unfair-practice facilitation principles recognized under the FTC Act, regardless of whether the merchant itself is separately pursued.

Issuer must prove (or obtain from the merchant) evidence of (i) agreed terms and (ii) actual delivery; absent that, denial is noncompliant and cannot rest on post-hoc assertions.



II.4 No Contract / No Assent / No Enforceable Terms

There is no enforceable agreement supporting the merchant’s claims:

No signed contract
No DocuSign
No click-wrap
No assent
No meeting of the minds
No agreement authorizing hourly billing
No agreement allowing changed terms
No agreement making the transaction non-refundable


Because no executed agreement exists authorizing hourly consulting, retroactive billing, modified deliverables, or non-refundability, the merchant’s asserted “109 hours at $45/hour” theory is a legal nullity. Under basic contract law principles, a party cannot impose material pricing terms, scope changes, or billing structures absent mutual assent. An unsigned, undisclosed, or unaccepted document - whether characterized as “terms,” “policies,” or “internal agreements” - has no legal force and cannot be enforced by the merchant or adopted by the issuer. Any attempt to retain funds on this basis constitutes retention without contractual or legal authority.



II.4A Required Proof to Sustain Denial (Must Be Produced)

Required proof to sustain denial (must be produced): (a) signed governing agreement; (b) signed hourly billing authorization; (c) itemized time records acknowledged by consumer; (d) proof of disclosure pre-payment; (e) proof of delivery of DFY outcomes; (f) full representment packet and case log.

Despite this, the bank:
Enforced unsigned terms
Shifted the burden of proof to the consumer
Accepted merchant allegations without proof
Denied access to rebuttal documents
Gave the merchant months to respond
Gave the consumer less than 10 days to respond - via a letter never received





II.5 Denial Basis = Procedure, Not Delivery

The denial was not based on evidence of delivery.


It was based on procedural technicalities and an unenforceable merchant narrative.
Maintaining denial under these circumstances, where the issuer has been provided overwhelming, documented evidence of deceptive inducement, non-delivery, lack of assent, and unenforceable terms - creates escalating regulatory exposure for First Interstate Bank. 

Under CFPB enforcement authority, issuers that engage in or perpetuate unfair, deceptive, or unreasonable dispute-resolution practices are subject to restitution orders, corrective action mandates, supervisory findings, and civil money penalties. 

CFPB civil penalty tiers currently authorize fines of up to $5,000 per day for standard violations, up to $25,000 per day for reckless violations, and up to $1,000,000 per day for knowing violations, with exposure increasing once an institution has actual notice and continues the challenged conduct. 

Administrative correction at this stage is the only resolution consistent with regulatory expectations and risk management principles.

Comparable issuer-side correction after escalation has been granted in prior CFPB-supervised billing error matters where provisional credit was ultimately made permanent following issuer escalation.


III. Issuer Liability, Penalties, and Litigation Exposure


If First Interstate Bank continues to enforce an unenforceable merchant narrative after notice, the bank faces escalating exposure under multiple independent authorities, including but not limited to:

1. Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666)
 • Statutory damages as authorized under the Truth in Lending Act and Fair Credit Billing Act civil liability provisions (15 U.S.C. §1640), together with:
 • Actual damages (interest, fees, credit harm, consequential loss)
	 • Mandatory attorney’s fees and costs

2. Regulation Z (12 C.F.R. §1026.13)
 • Civil liability for failure to investigate
 • Liability for defective notice and explanation
 • Liability for unlawful collection activity during dispute

3. CFPB UDAAP Authority (12 U.S.C. §§5531–5536)
 Civil money penalties of:
 - Up to $5,000 per day (standard violations)
 - Up to $25,000 per day (reckless violations)
 -  Up to $1,000,000 per day (knowing violations)
 - Restitution orders
 - Corrective action mandates
 - Supervisory findings and compliance downgrades

4. FTC Act §5 (15 U.S.C. §45)
 • Liability for facilitating or perpetuating unfair or deceptive practices once on notice
 • Issuer responsibility where enforcement enables merchant deception
Based on the documented timeline, repeated notice, and continued enforcement, First Interstate Bank’s exposure substantially exceeds the amount in dispute. Correction at this stage materially reduces regulatory, litigation, and reputational risk.

This is why this matter involves:
Fraudulent inducement
Material misrepresentation
Lack of mutual assent

Willful or reckless disregard once notice was provided

Willful misconduct

### **III.A Operational, Network, and Supervisory Exposure (Primary Executive Risk)**

> Independent of statutory penalties, continued enforcement of this denial creates immediate **issuer-side operational and supervisory exposure** that materially exceeds the amount in dispute.
>
> **Card-network dispute rules (Visa / Mastercard) require issuers to base denials on proof of (i) agreed terms and (ii) actual delivery.** Where a merchant relies on undisclosed, unsigned, or retroactively imposed terms — and where delivery of the promised service did not occur — issuers may not lawfully sustain a denial or shift the burden to the cardholder.
>
> By upholding this denial **after documented notice of non-delivery, lack of assent, and unenforceable terms**, the bank assumes **direct issuer responsibility** for:
>
> • A defective investigation finding  
> • Improper reliance on merchant assertions  
> • Network-rule noncompliance  
> • Loss allocation shifting from merchant to issuer  
> • Audit and supervisory exception risk  
>
> Once a billing error dispute reaches this posture, **continued enforcement converts a merchant dispute into an issuer-side compliance failure**. At that point, loss exposure is no longer capped by the transaction amount and becomes:
>
> • An internal loss event  
> • A compliance exception subject to supervisory review  
> • A precedent case impacting future disputes  
> • A documented failure of Reg Z investigation standards  
>
> Issuer-side correction at this stage is the **standard risk-mitigation response** used to avoid locking in a provable investigation defect that survives escalation, audit, or litigation review.
>
> **This is why similarly situated issuers routinely reverse and make provisional credit permanent after escalation — not because of merchant fault, but because issuer-side exposure materially exceeds the disputed amount.**

### **I.0 Summary of Issuer Violations & Unavoidable Liability Exposure**

Based on the documented record, First Interstate Bank has already incurred **multiple, independent violations of federal billing-error and consumer-protection law**, each of which carries **separate and cumulative liability**.

These violations include, but are not limited to:

**A. Failure to Conduct a Reasonable Investigation**  
(15 U.S.C. §1666(a); 12 C.F.R. §1026.13(c))  
— sustaining a denial without proof of agreed terms or actual delivery.

**B. Improper Reliance on Merchant Assertions**  
(Reg Z; CFPB UDAAP standards)  
— accepting an undisclosed, unsigned, retroactive billing theory in direct contradiction of the documented transaction.

**C. Defective Denial and Withholding of Supporting Documentation**  
(15 U.S.C. §1666(a)(3)(B); 12 C.F.R. §1026.13(f))  
— failing to provide a denial that meaningfully addresses the consumer’s evidence and refusing access to the representment record.

**D. Procedural Unfairness and Burden Shifting**  
(CFPB UDAAP; FTC Act §5 principles)  
— depriving the consumer of a meaningful opportunity to respond while granting the merchant extended deadlines.

Each of the above violations independently triggers **civil liability under the Truth in Lending Act (15 U.S.C. §1640)**, including:

• Statutory damages  
• Actual damages  
• Mandatory attorney’s fees and costs  

Once notice was provided and enforcement continued, exposure escalated from negligent to **knowing or reckless noncompliance**, materially increasing regulatory, supervisory, and litigation risk.

**If litigated, liability does not depend on disputed facts or credibility determinations.**  
It is established by the bank’s own investigation posture, documented record, and continued reliance on unenforceable merchant assertions after notice.

Administrative correction at this stage avoids locking in a provable issuer-side compliance failure that would otherwise survive regulatory, audit, and judicial review.

## 🤖 ChatGPT

Good question — this matters more than most people realize.

Here’s the **clean, correct, low-risk way** to sign, modeled on what works in issuer/legal escalations like the NF win.

---

## Short Answer (what to do)

**Sign in your individual capacity, with rights reserved.**  
Do **not** sign “without recourse.”  
Only include a business name **if it was the cardholder or contracting party**.

---

## Why “WITHOUT RECOURSE” is the wrong move here

“Without recourse” is typically used to:
- disclaim liability on negotiable instruments, or
- limit future claims

In **dispute / litigation posture**, it can:
- muddy standing,
- invite arguments about waiver, or
- be mischaracterized as limiting remedies.

👉 **You want the opposite**: maximum preservation of rights.

---

## The Correct Sign-Off (Recommended)

### ✅ Use this — clean, standard, strong

```
Respectfully,

[Your Full Legal Name]  
Cardholder / Consumer  
All rights reserved.  
```

That’s it.

This:
- preserves **individual standing**
- aligns with FCBA / Reg Z posture
- avoids waiver arguments
- mirrors successful issuer escalations

---

## If a Business Was Involved (only if true)

Only do this **if the card was issued to the business or the contract names the business**.

```
Respectfully,

[Your Full Legal Name]  
Authorized Representative, [Business Legal Name]  
Cardholder / Consumer  
All rights reserved.  
```

Do **not** add EINs, titles beyond “Authorized Representative,” or extra language unless asked.

---

## What NOT to add

❌ “Without recourse”  
❌ “Under protest”  
❌ Long UCC citations  
❌ Sovereign / pseudo-legal language  
❌ Threatening closers (“failure to comply will result in…”)

Your letter already does the work.  
The signature should **not** compete with it.

```
[Your Full Legal Name]

All rights reserved as to facts, law, and remedies.  
```

- **Individual name first**
- **Rights reserved**
- **No “without recourse”**
- **Business name only if legally necessary**

## **Amicable Resolution & Non-Retaliation Notice**

This correspondence is made in **good faith** and with the **express intent to resolve this matter quickly and amicably** through administrative correction, without litigation or further regulatory escalation.

Nothing herein is intended as a threat, ultimatum, or waiver of rights. Rather, this notice is provided to afford First Interstate Bank a full and fair opportunity to cure the identified violations and mitigate exposure consistent with federal law and supervisory expectations.

The undersigned expressly **reserves all rights, claims, remedies, and causes of action**, whether legal or equitable, and whether known or unknown at this time. No rights are waived by seeking administrative resolution.

### **Non-Retaliation Notice**
Pursuant to the Fair Credit Billing Act, Regulation Z, and federal UDAAP standards, First Interstate Bank is prohibited from engaging in any form of retaliation or adverse action in response to a good-faith billing error dispute or the exercise of statutory rights, including but not limited to:

- Interest or fee accrual related to the disputed amount  
- Negative credit reporting  
- Account restriction, closure, or adverse treatment  
- Collection activity or escalation  
- Any action that could reasonably be construed as punitive or coercive  

Any such conduct after notice will constitute **independent and additional violations**, subject to separate regulatory and civil exposure.

Administrative correction at this stage will fully resolve the matter and is the most efficient outcome for all parties.

This letter is not sent in anticipation of dispute, but to afford the issuer a final administrative opportunity to correct the billing error and mitigate escalating regulatory and litigation exposure.
Administrative resolution at this stage is the most efficient and least disruptive outcome.


I. Statutory Basis & Issuer Exposure (Read First)
II. Plain-English Executive Summary
III. Issuer Liability, Penalties, and Litigation Exposure
IV. Master Legal & Compliance Checklist

I. Statutory Basis & Issuer Exposure 
(Read First)

This matter arises from First Interstate Bank’s mishandling of a federally protected billing error dispute, in violation of the Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666), Regulation Z (12 C.F.R. §1026.13), and federal UDAAP standards enforced by the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau.
I.A. Trigger Event - Notice of Billing Error
 • Once First Interstate Bank received notice of a billing error, the bank assumed independent, non-delegable statutory duties.
I.B. Duty 1 - Reasonable Investigation
 • Conduct a reasonable investigation (§1666(a); Reg Z §1026.13(c))
I.C. Duty 2 - No Collection / Adverse Action During Dispute
 • Refrain from collection or adverse action during the dispute (§1666(a)(3)(B)(ii))
I.D. Duty 3 - Written Explanation + Supporting Documentation
 • Provide a written explanation of any denial addressing the consumer’s evidence (§1666(a)(3)(B); Reg Z §1026.13(f)) and provide supporting documentation upon request.
I.E. Duty 4 - No Reliance on Merchant Assertions / Unenforceable Policies
 • Avoid reliance on merchant assertions or unenforceable policies

Failure to meet these duties exposes the issuer to statutory damages, actual damages, attorney’s fees, regulatory enforcement, supervisory findings, and civil money penalties. These obligations apply regardless of merchant refund policies, alleged service hours, or internal merchant narratives.


I.0 Summary of Issuer Violations & Unavoidable Liability Exposure
Based on the documented record, First Interstate Bank has already incurred multiple, independent violations of federal billing-error and consumer-protection law, each of which carries separate and cumulative liability.
These violations include, but are not limited to:
A. Failure to Conduct a Reasonable Investigation
 (15 U.S.C. §1666(a); 12 C.F.R. §1026.13(c))
 - sustaining a denial without proof of agreed terms or actual delivery.
B. Improper Reliance on Merchant Assertions
 (Reg Z; CFPB UDAAP standards)
 - accepting an undisclosed, unsigned, retroactive billing theory in direct contradiction of the documented transaction.
C. Defective Denial and Withholding of Supporting Documentation
 (15 U.S.C. §1666(a)(3)(B); 12 C.F.R. §1026.13(f))
 - failing to provide a denial that meaningfully addresses the consumer’s evidence and refusing access to the representment record.
D. Procedural Unfairness and Burden Shifting
 (CFPB UDAAP; FTC Act §5 principles)
 - depriving the consumer of a meaningful opportunity to respond while granting the merchant extended deadlines.
Each of the above violations independently triggers civil liability under the Truth in Lending Act (15 U.S.C. §1640), including:
• Statutory damages
 • Actual damages
 • Mandatory attorney’s fees and costs
Once notice was provided and enforcement continued, exposure escalated from negligent to knowing or reckless noncompliance, materially increasing regulatory, supervisory, and litigation risk.
If litigated, liability does not depend on disputed facts or credibility determinations.
 It is established by the bank’s own investigation posture, documented record, and continued reliance on unenforceable merchant assertions after notice.
Administrative correction at this stage avoids locking in a provable issuer-side compliance failure that would otherwise survive regulatory, audit, and judicial review.

I.0.1 Liability Is Inevitable if Litigation or Regulatory Review Occurs
If this matter proceeds to litigation or regulatory adjudication, issuer liability is unavoidable based on the existing record.
This outcome does not depend on disputed facts, witness credibility, or competing narratives. It flows directly from:
• The bank’s failure to conduct a reasonable investigation after notice
 • Reliance on an undisclosed, unsigned, and retroactively imposed merchant billing theory
 • Enforcement of non-existent contractual terms
 • Procedural deprivation of the consumer’s statutory response rights
 • Continued denial after actual notice of non-delivery and lack of assent
Once notice was provided, continued enforcement converted the issue from a merchant dispute into an issuer-side compliance violation under the FCBA, Regulation Z, and CFPB-enforced UDAAP standards.
Courts and regulators do not permit issuers to avoid liability by deferring to merchant assertions where:
 (a) no enforceable agreement exists, and
 (b) promised outcomes were never delivered.
Accordingly, liability attaches as a matter of law, not discretion.
Administrative correction at this stage is not a concession - it is the only action consistent with statutory compliance, risk containment, and supervisory expectations.



II. PLAIN-ENGLISH EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

(this frames everything that follows)

This dispute is not just “services not rendered.”

It is a pattern of misrepresentation, lack of agreement, retroactive billing, and procedural unfairness, compounded by the bank’s failure to conduct a reasonable investigation. There is a pattern of conduct that, once notice was provided, supports findings of willful non-compliance, willful negligence, willful misconduct, and reckless disregard.



II.1 What Was Purchased & Promised

I purchased a Done-For-You (DFY) Airbnb service, NOT consulting.
The merchant promised (verified in already provided documentation: ads, website, sales calls, written materials, graphics, and transcripts) to:


Acquire a property
Furnish it
List it
Deliver a cash-flowing Airbnb generating ~$3,500-$5,000+/month
With explicit representations such as: “If we don’t acquire you a property, you pay $0.”
Explicit week by week timeline of deliverables 


None of that happened. (all proven and verified factually and indisputably via already provided ads, website, sales calls, written materials, graphics, and transcripts)


No property acquired
No lease signed
No furnishing
No listing
No revenue




II.2 Post-Failure Recharacterization - Non-Delivery Downscale, Partial Refund, and Unenforceable Retroactive Billing (109 Hours / $45 per Hour)

Documented & Provided Record confirms non-delivery and downscale/partial refund: The merchant acknowledged missed timelines/non-delivery and agreed to a downscale (from two properties to one) and issued a partial refund of $7,500 (as documented in the provided texts/emails).

After continued non-delivery, a billing error dispute was initiated: After the merchant still failed to deliver the promised DFY (Done-For-You) outcome, the consumer initiated billing-error/chargeback remedies based on misrepresentation, lack of assent, and non-delivery. (Documentation already provided multiple times)
Following the partial refund and downscale, the consumer granted the merchant an additional extension and opportunity to deliver the remaining single promised property. Despite this extension and additional time beyond the original and revised timelines, the merchant failed to acquire, furnish, list, or deliver even one revenue-producing Airbnb property as promised, marketed, advertised, and transcribed. This extended non-delivery is documented in the record.

In response to the chargeback claim, following their second opportunity for failed delivery of promised and fraudulently induced misrepresented deliverables of a DFY Airbnb profiting , furnished, & listed property, the merchant responds that:
They retroactively claimed the money was for “consulting”
They alleged 109 hours at $45/hour to justify retaining $10,500 (unsupported merchant allegation; unproven, undocumented, and never consented to) - notwithstanding that the consumer’s participation consisted of occasional, optionally scheduled weekly calls of approximately one hour.
The asserted “109 hours at $45/hour” figure is not a term agreed to by the consumer, nor is it supported by any executed agreement, disclosure, or consent. This figure is an unsupported allegation made by the merchant and relayed by the bank, introduced only after the merchant failed to deliver the promised Done-For-You Airbnb service. At no point did the consumer agree to hourly consulting, time-based billing, or any pricing structure contingent on hours worked rather than outcome delivered. The consumer’s actual participation consisted of occasional weekly update calls of approximately one hour, which were ancillary to the promised DFY service and never represented or disclosed as billable consulting.

No contemporaneous time records, invoices, or consumer-acknowledged logs have been produced.

This hourly billing:
Was never disclosed
Was never agreed to
Was never signed
Was never part of the deal


In reality, I only had occasional weekly calls (~1 hour each), which were calls to keep me up to date, not paid hourly consulting.



II.3 Net Impression + Issuer UDAAP Exposure

Under FTC Act §5, the transaction is defined by the net impression of the merchant’s advertising and sales representations. 

All representations in this matter concerned a Done-For-You Airbnb acquisition and handover, not hourly consulting. 

The merchant’s post-hoc claim of ‘109 hours at $45/hour’ was never disclosed, never agreed to, and is materially inconsistent with the advertised deliverables, including express representations that no payment would be owed absent property acquisition. (unsupported merchant allegation; no signed agreement, disclosure, or consumer consent exists)

The FTC treats such retroactive recharacterization as deceptive, unreasonable, and unenforceable. Reliance on unsigned or undisclosed terms cannot cure or override deceptive advertising.

These FTC Act §5 standards are directly applicable to First Interstate Bank as the issuer responsible for conducting a reasonable investigation and for avoiding unfair or deceptive practices in dispute resolution. 

Once the bank is placed on notice that a merchant’s post-transaction narrative is materially inconsistent with the net impression of the advertising and sales representations, the bank may not lawfully rely on that narrative to deny a billing error claim. 

II.3A) Compliance

Accepting and enforcing an undisclosed, unsigned, and retroactively imposed billing theory, particularly one that contradicts express outcome-based promises; exposes the bank to liability for facilitating or perpetuating an unfair practice, failing to conduct a reasonable investigation, and improperly shifting the burden of proof to the consumer. 

Federal regulators, including the CFPB, evaluate such conduct as an independent compliance failure, regardless of merchant misconduct, where the issuer disregards clear evidence of deceptive inducement, lack of assent, and non-delivery. 
Continued enforcement after notice materially increases regulatory exposure, corrective action risk, restitution obligations, and supervisory findings.


II.3B) Notice

Once First Interstate Bank was placed on actual notice of the material misrepresentations, lack of mutual assent, unenforceable and unsigned terms, and total non-delivery of the promised Done-For-You Airbnb service, the bank assumed an independent legal obligation to correct the billing error and conduct a reasonable, evidence-based investigation. At that point, continued reliance on the merchant’s post-hoc narrative, particularly one that contradicts the net impression of the merchant’s own advertising and sales representations - constitutes a separate and independent compliance failure by the issuer. 

Issuer obligations under the FCBA and Regulation Z are non-delegable and cannot be satisfied by deferring to merchant assertions where the evidentiary record contradicts those assertions.

Federal regulators do not permit issuers to defer to merchant assertions where the record demonstrates deceptive inducement, non-delivery, and lack of consumer consent. Continued enforcement after notice exposes the bank to issuer-side liability under Regulation Z, UDAAP standards enforced by the CFPB, and unfair-practice facilitation principles recognized under the FTC Act, regardless of whether the merchant itself is separately pursued.

Issuer must prove (or obtain from the merchant) evidence of (i) agreed terms and (ii) actual delivery; absent that, denial is noncompliant and cannot rest on post-hoc assertions.



II.4 No Contract / No Assent / No Enforceable Terms

There is no enforceable agreement supporting the merchant’s claims:

No signed contract
No DocuSign
No click-wrap
No assent
No meeting of the minds
No agreement authorizing hourly billing
No agreement allowing changed terms
No agreement making the transaction non-refundable


Because no executed agreement exists authorizing hourly consulting, retroactive billing, modified deliverables, or non-refundability, the merchant’s asserted “109 hours at $45/hour” theory is a legal nullity. Under basic contract law principles, a party cannot impose material pricing terms, scope changes, or billing structures absent mutual assent. An unsigned, undisclosed, or unaccepted document - whether characterized as “terms,” “policies,” or “internal agreements” - has no legal force and cannot be enforced by the merchant or adopted by the issuer. Any attempt to retain funds on this basis constitutes retention without contractual or legal authority.



II.4A Required Proof to Sustain Denial (Must Be Produced)

Required proof to sustain denial (must be produced): (a) signed governing agreement; (b) signed hourly billing authorization; (c) itemized time records acknowledged by consumer; (d) proof of disclosure pre-payment; (e) proof of delivery of DFY outcomes; (f) full representment packet and case log.

Despite this, the bank:
Enforced unsigned terms
Shifted the burden of proof to the consumer
Accepted merchant allegations without proof
Denied access to rebuttal documents
Gave the merchant months to respond
Gave the consumer less than 10 days to respond - via a letter never received





II.5 Denial Basis = Procedure, Not Delivery

The denial was not based on evidence of delivery.


It was based on procedural technicalities and an unenforceable merchant narrative.
Maintaining denial under these circumstances, where the issuer has been provided overwhelming, documented evidence of deceptive inducement, non-delivery, lack of assent, and unenforceable terms - creates escalating regulatory exposure for First Interstate Bank. 

Under CFPB enforcement authority, issuers that engage in or perpetuate unfair, deceptive, or unreasonable dispute-resolution practices are subject to restitution orders, corrective action mandates, supervisory findings, and civil money penalties. 

CFPB civil penalty tiers currently authorize fines of up to $5,000 per day for standard violations, up to $25,000 per day for reckless violations, and up to $1,000,000 per day for knowing violations, with exposure increasing once an institution has actual notice and continues the challenged conduct. 

Administrative correction at this stage is the only resolution consistent with regulatory expectations and risk management principles.

Comparable issuer-side correction after escalation has been granted in prior CFPB-supervised billing error matters where provisional credit was ultimately made permanent following issuer escalation.


III. Issuer Liability, Penalties, and Litigation Exposure


If First Interstate Bank continues to enforce an unenforceable merchant narrative after notice, the bank faces escalating exposure under multiple independent authorities, including but not limited to:

1. Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666)
 • Statutory damages as authorized under the Truth in Lending Act and Fair Credit Billing Act civil liability provisions (15 U.S.C. §1640), together with:
 • Actual damages (interest, fees, credit harm, consequential loss)
	 • Mandatory attorney’s fees and costs

2. Regulation Z (12 C.F.R. §1026.13)
 • Civil liability for failure to investigate
 • Liability for defective notice and explanation
 • Liability for unlawful collection activity during dispute

3. CFPB UDAAP Authority (12 U.S.C. §§5531–5536)
 Civil money penalties of:
 - Up to $5,000 per day (standard violations)
 - Up to $25,000 per day (reckless violations)
 -  Up to $1,000,000 per day (knowing violations)
 - Restitution orders
 - Corrective action mandates
 - Supervisory findings and compliance downgrades

4. FTC Act §5 (15 U.S.C. §45)
 • Liability for facilitating or perpetuating unfair or deceptive practices once on notice
 • Issuer responsibility where enforcement enables merchant deception
Based on the documented timeline, repeated notice, and continued enforcement, First Interstate Bank’s exposure substantially exceeds the amount in dispute. Correction at this stage materially reduces regulatory, litigation, and reputational risk.

This is why this matter involves:
Fraudulent inducement
Material misrepresentation
Lack of mutual assent
Retroactive billing
Unfair and unreasonable investigation practices
Willful or reckless disregard once notice was provided
Willful non-compliance
Willful misconduct

III.A Operational, Network, and Supervisory Exposure (Primary Executive Risk)
Independent of statutory penalties, continued enforcement of this denial creates immediate issuer-side operational and supervisory exposure that materially exceeds the amount in dispute.

Card-network dispute rules (Visa / Mastercard) require issuers to base denials on proof of (i) agreed terms and (ii) actual delivery. Where a merchant relies on undisclosed, unsigned, or retroactively imposed terms - and where delivery of the promised service did not occur - issuers may not lawfully sustain a denial or shift the burden to the cardholder.

By upholding this denial after documented notice of non-delivery, lack of assent, and unenforceable terms, the bank assumes direct issuer responsibility for:
• A defective investigation finding
 • Improper reliance on merchant assertions
 • Network-rule noncompliance
 • Loss allocation shifting from merchant to issuer
 • Audit and supervisory exception risk

Once a billing error dispute reaches this posture, continued enforcement converts a merchant dispute into an issuer-side compliance failure. At that point, loss exposure is no longer capped by the transaction amount and becomes:

• An internal loss event
 • A compliance exception subject to supervisory review
 • A precedent case impacting future disputes
 • A documented failure of Reg Z investigation standards

Issuer-side correction at this stage is the standard risk-mitigation response used to avoid locking in a provable investigation defect that survives escalation, audit, or litigation review.
This is why similarly situated issuers routinely reverse and make provisional credit permanent after escalation - not because of merchant fault, but because issuer-side exposure materially exceeds the disputed amount.

Where a merchant asserts alternative deliverables, modified pricing, or non-refundability, the merchant and issuer must prove the existence of a valid, enforceable agreement reflecting those terms. The consumer is not required to disprove unsupported allegations or reconstruct a transaction that was never agreed to. The record already contains extensive affirmative proof establishing the original DFY scope, the promised outcomes, and the absence of delivery.

Absent production of a signed agreement expressly authorizing alternative pricing or deliverables, no further rebuttal is legally required from the consumer.

> What product or service is your complaint about?
> What type of problem are you having?
> What happened?
> Regulation Z & UDAAP Noncompliance - Failure to Investigate and Ongoing Violations This complaint concerns First Interstate
> Bank’s failure to conduct a reasonable investigation, and its willful misconduct, willful negligence, reckless disregard, and willful
> noncompliance with Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13) and Dodd-Frank UDAAP standards in handling my credit card billing
> dispute. This submission is not a duplicate of CFPB Case 260131-28391819. That complaint addressed the underlying transaction.
> This complaint addresses the bank’s procedural and substantive violations during and after the dispute process. First Interstate
> Bank denied a services-not-rendered dispute without identifying any evidence that the contracted service was delivered. No
> property was delivered, no listing launched, no revenue generated, and the merchant issued a partial refund acknowledging non￾delivery. Despite this undisputed record, the bank denied the dispute and continues to charge interest. The bank relied on
> unsigned, post-payment merchant terms, refused to provide the merchant’s rebuttal documentation, and refused to disclose the
> dates the rebuttal was received, processed, or reviewed, preventing verification of compliance with Regulation Z. The bank gave
> the merchant approximately 60-90+ days to respond (Aug 13, 2025 to Nov 13, 2025), while giving me fewer than 10 days, by
> physical mail only, despite knowing I was traveling and after I requested digital delivery. I have never received the alleged
> November 14, 2025 letter, yet the dispute was closed anyway. The bank now claims it “cannot assist” because internal timelines
> have passed. This is legally false. Regulation Z provides no safe harbor for denying a billing error based on procedure when the
> investigation is unreasonable or when the underlying facts establish non-delivery. A procedurally timely denial that ratifies
> merchant non-performance is not compliance - it is facilitation. Temporary access to: AirDNA, marketplaces, or consultation does
> not constitute delivery of the promised DFY merchant services with an abundance of proof provided on the subject in detail,
> directly from the merchant themselves. Under Mastercard Reason Code 4853, the test is binary: Was the promised service
> delivered? Here, the answer is no. The promised service with provided proof explicitly states a DFY (Done-For-You) Airbnb
> acquired property; designed, furnished, & listed that profits $3,500-$5,000+/mo after operational expenses. Token or preparatory
> activity cannot cure total non-delivery. No timeline argument can cure that defect. First Interstate Bank continues to demonstrate
> willful negligence, and routinely ignores action relating to directly addressed compliance with UDAAP, REG Z, proper
> investigation, etc. They refuse to answer compliance related questions, and continue to violate consumer chargeback rights, and
> are abusing their powers as a bank, due to a lack of due diligence, and repeated documented willful misconduct. I am requesting
> immediate supervisory review and administrative correction. These Regulation Z and UDAAP violations persist after notice and
> appeal, warranting CFPB oversight. This must be escalated to all relevant enforcement agencies.
> What would be a fair resolution to this issue?
> 1. Immediate reversal of the remaining $10,500 charge for services not rendered. 2. Removal and refund of all interest, fees, and finance charges
> accrued on the disputed amount. 3. Written confirmation that the account balance has been corrected and no negative credit reporting occurred or
> will occur. 4. Disclosure of the complete merchant representment package relied upon, including the exact dates the rebuttal was received,
> processed, and reviewed. 5. Written confirmation that First Interstate Bank has corrected its Regulation Z dispute handling deficiencies in this
> matter. 6. No retaliation 7. Compliance with consumer rights, UDAAP, Reg Z, & chargeback rights. 8. Retraining of First Interstate Bank
> procedures relating to proper due process, investigations, appeals, and consumer rights relative to chargeback services.
> 10 attachments
> View uploaded documents by clicking on the file name. Documents that pass virus scanning are typically available within 2 minutes of upload.
