> Rev. 12/04/23 Complaint Information Page 1 of 4 
> STATE OF MONTANA  DEPARTMENT OF ADMINISTRATION
> DIVISION OF BANKING AND FINANCIAL INSTITUTIONS
> 301 South Park Ave, Suite 316  PO Box 200546  Helena, MT 59620-0546 
> Phone: 406-841-2920  Fax: 406-841-2930 
> Website: www.banking.mt.gov  E-Mail: banking@mt.gov
> COMPLAINT INFORMATION 
> WHEN SHOULD I FILE A COMPLAINT WITH THE DIVISION? 
> Before filing a complaint with the Division, consumers are encouraged to try to resolve the 
> dispute themselves. We have found that complaints can normally be resolved if the consumer 
> contacts the business directly. If you have not already done so, please contact the office
> manager of the business and attempt to resolve the problems yourself. If direct contact is 
> unsuccessful, or if you are dissatisfied with the results, please complete the complaint form, and 
> send it to the Division. Your complaint will be promptly acknowledged, and you will be 
> advised of any action we take.
> IMPORTANT THINGS TO KNOW ABOUT THE DIVISION’S VOLUNTARY COMPLAINT PROCESS: 
>  The Division can only act on violations of laws, rules, and regulations by businesses
> regulated by the Division.
>  The Division cannot give you legal advice, nor can we recommend an attorney to you.
>  The Division cannot pursue legal redress on your behalf. You should always contact a
> private attorney who can advise you of your rights.
>  The Division cannot handle a complaint that is in active litigation and has no authority to
> provide any extrajudicial relief regarding any issue that has previously been litigated or
> settled. If you have an attorney related to the issues in any complaint, you must provide
> your attorney’s information so that the Division can communicate with your legal
> counsel.
>  The Division can only accept a complaint from the person involved in the dispute or the
> legal representative of that person.
> FREQUENTLY ASKED QUESTIONS
> What if the Division doesn’t have authority?
> In situations where the Division determines that it does not hold authority to investigate, we will 
> 
> <PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 2 / 7>
> 
> Rev. 12/04/23 Complaint Information Page 2 of 4 
> either refer your complaint to the appropriate authority or reject your complaint with an 
> explanation. If you do not understand our rejection or referral, please call our office for further 
> information.
> In some cases, the Division may initially believe that it holds jurisdiction over the issues raised, 
> but upon further investigation may determine that it does not. You will be informed of any such 
> determination and again may call the Division for clarification.
> How does the process work?
> You, the consumer, will be referred to as “consumer” throughout the investigation and 
> resolution. Upon receipt of your complaint, the information is logged into our complaint 
> database and notification of receipt is sent to you. If the Division needs additional information, 
> we will contact you and make a request. If you provide accurate initial information, it will make 
> the process more efficient.
> The Division next notifies the business, referred to as “Respondent”, that a complaint has been 
> received and provides them with a copy of the complaint with any attached information. The 
> business is instructed to respond to the complaint and provide information within 30 days. 
> Once the business has responded to the Division, the facts, evidence, and issues will be reviewed 
> in relation to the appropriate laws and regulations. The Division will prepare a recommended 
> resolution to the complaint which is sent to the consumer and business for consideration. Please 
> keep in mind, this process can take several weeks depending on the complexity of issues in your 
> complaint.
> Must you comply with the resolution?
> No. You are not under the Division’s jurisdiction, and the Division cannot provide you with legal 
> advice.
> Are your rights limited by the resolution?
> No. You have the right to pursue the matter yourself or with legal counsel regardless of how the 
> Division handles the matter. Please note that the Division cannot act privately on your behalf. 
> HOW DO I COMPLETE THE COMPLAINT FORM? 
> Answer all questions and complete the questions as accurately as possible. Please keep these 
> things in mind:
>  Be logical, fair, and objective. The business will review your complaint description.
> Our experience is that inflammatory statements or derogatory comments only cause to
> 
> <PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 3 / 7>
> 
> Rev. 12/04/23 Complaint Information Page 3 of 4 
> further distance between the parties and make a satisfactory resolution more difficult.
>  Be concise. The fewer words you can use to describe the problem the better. Stick to
> the relevant facts. For example, it is important for the Division to know when you
> applied for a loan, but not necessarily the personal reasons why you needed a loan.
>  Who, What, Where, When, Why, and How. Don’t forget these clarifying facts.
> Especially the dates! In the regulatory industry, dates are of paramount importance. If
> you have an accurate recollection of the day you received a specific disclosure or
> document… tell us. Did you retain something that will support this date? Show us.
> Who did what? Where were they when it happened? Remember, we must form an
> understanding based upon the facts you provide us, and then we must attempt to verify
> these facts.
>  Documents are the key to any financial transaction. Carefully review the documents
> you were given and provide legible copies of those that support your claim to us. We
> are especially interested in disclosures or written statements you may have received from
> the Respondent. If possible, please send copies of your documentation electronically. If
> you must send hard-copies - do NOT send originals unless we request them. Also, please
> redact any personal information such as social security numbers, date of birth, etc...
>  Be reasonable in your request for a resolution to the matter. The Division will make
> its resolution request based in law and regulation. On occasion, after the Respondent
> reviews the complaint, a resolution may be obtained before the Division even begins its
> investigation.
> HOW DO I FILE MY COMPLAINT? 
> A complaint form can be downloaded from our website at www.banking.mt.gov. (Note: This 
> form must be downloaded and saved to your computer before filling out and clicking the submit 
> button.) 
> IS YOUR COMPLAINT CONFIDENTIAL? 
> Generally, no. The documents and information you and Respondent provide may be provided 
> to the interested parties in the complaint; law enforcement, the Department of Justice, or the 
> county attorney. If the Division refers your complaint to another agency, our policies do not 
> govern the dissemination of requested items by that agency. Likewise, the Respondent is not 
> required to comply with the Division’s policy on records production. 
> WHAT THE DIVISION CAN DO FOR YOU
> • The Division can advise you of the documentation you should expect to receive
> throughout the course of your transaction.
> 
> <PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 4 / 7>
> 
> Rev. 12/04/23 Complaint Information Page 4 of 4 
> • The Division can contact the business and request a written response to your complaint.
> WHAT THE DIVISION CANNOT DO FOR YOU: 
> • The Division cannot give you legal advice, nor can we recommend an attorney to you.
> • The Division cannot pursue redress on your behalf. You should always contact a private
> attorney who can advise you of your rights.
> • The Division cannot obtain records on any account on which you are not a signatory.
> CONTACT INFORMATION
> If you have any questions, you may contact the Division at (406) 841-2920 or
> banking@mt.gov. If you have documentation from the Division concerning your complaint, 
> please reference the complaint number for greater efficiency in response. 
>

>

> Improvement Act (15 U.S.C. §57a(f)). The FDIC will use this information to respond to your questions and requests for assistance involving activities or practices of
> FDIC-insured depository institutions. Submitting this information to the FDIC is voluntary. Failure to submit all of the information requested and to complete the form
> entirely could delay or prevent the response to your request. The information provided by individuals is protected by the Privacy Act, 5 USC §552a, as amended. The
> information may be furnished to the institution which is the subject of the complaint or inquiry; to the Federal or State supervisory authority that has direct
> supervision over the financial institution; to appropriate Federal, state, local or foreign law enforcement authorities; to a court, administrative tribunal, or a party in
> litigation; to contractors, agents and other third parties as authorized by law, and in accordance with any of the other routine uses described in the FDIC Consumer
> Complaint and Inquiry Records (FDIC-005) System of Records. A complete copy of this System of Records Notice is available at https://www.fdic.gov/privacy
> (https://www.fdic.gov/privacy). If you have questions or concerns about the collection or use of the information, you may contact the FDIC’s Chief Privacy Officer at

> This complaint concerns First Interstate Bank’s handling of a credit card billing dispute involving services not rendered, including the bank’s investigation process,

> The transaction was for a Done-For-You Airbnb service promising acquisition of fully furnished and designed properties, setup, listing, and revenue generation of
> $3,500-$5,000+ per month per property, for two properties.
> - No property or lease was ever obtained.
> - No Airbnb listing ever went live.
> - No revenue was ever generated.
> - The merchant later issued a partial refund, acknowledging non-delivery of the contracted service.
> Dispute timeline and bank actions:
> August 13, 2025: I opened a billing dispute with First Interstate Bank for services not rendered.
> September 5, 2025: I submitted complete dispute documentation to the bank.
> September 25, 2025: The bank issued provisional credit.
> November 13, 2025: The bank states that the merchant submitted a rebuttal more than 90 days after the dispute was opened.
> November 14, 2025: The bank claims it mailed a notice requesting a response; I did not receive this notice.

> I am a Minnesota consumer submitting this complaint regarding Steam and Stream and its operator, Khaleel
> Gonzalez, for deceptive trade practices, fraudulent inducement, and theft by deception.
> On or about June 4, 2025, I paid $18,000 for a “Done-For-You” Airbnb acquisition and deployment service. The
> merchant expressly represented - publicly and in writing - that they would secure a property, launch a live Airbnb
> listing, and generate rental income. The merchant also stated in writing that if no property was obtained, I would
> owe $0.
> None of the promised services were delivered.
> No property was acquired.
> No lease was executed.
> No furnishing was done.
> No listing went live.
> No revenue was generated.
> After months of non-delivery, the merchant issued a partial refund of $7,500, acknowledging failure to perform, but
> retained $10,500 without providing any service. The merchant then attempted to justify keeping the remaining funds
> by relying on unsigned, post-payment “terms” that were never disclosed, never agreed to, and never signed.
> Despite being directly presented with his own written “you pay $0 if we don’t get you a property” representation and
> the undisputed fact that no property was ever obtained, the merchant refused to refund the remaining balance and
> instructed me to stop contacting him.
> This conduct goes beyond a contract dispute and reflects knowing retention of money obtained through
> misrepresentation. I am currently appealing a related chargeback and have an open CFPB complaint regarding the
> bank’s handling of this transaction. In addition to an FTC report, and Texas AG report opened on Khaleel Gonzalez
> and his company Steam and Stream.
> I am requesting that the Minnesota Attorney General review this matter for violations of consumer protection laws,
> investigate whether this conduct reflects a broader pattern affecting other consumers, and pursue appropriate
> enforcement, including restitution.
> What do you want the company to do? *
> I request investigation, restitution of the remaining $10,500 plus interest, in addition to work and time loss, travel fees,
> and any enforcement action necessary to prevent further consumer harm.

> Brief Description of Complaint (Texas Attorney General)
> I am submitting this complaint against Steam and Stream and its operator Khaleel Gonzalez for fraudulent inducement,
> deceptive trade practices, and theft by deception arising from a Done-For-You (“DFY”) Airbnb investment service that was
> never delivered.
> In or around 06/04/2025, I paid $18,000 to Steam and Stream & Khaleel Gonzalez - based on express representations that
> the merchant would secure a lease, set up an Airbnb property, launch a live listing, and generate rental income, with
> public and private claims including $10,000+ per month revenue and $3,500-$5,000+ monthly profit per property
> starting out, with the schedule to pay off the furnishings and the cost of the program in 7-10 months via the profits after
> expenses. The merchant further represented in writing that if a property was not obtained, I would owe $0.
> None of the promised core services were ever delivered.
> No lease was acquired.
> No property was secured.
> Nothing was furnished.
> No Airbnb listing ever went live.
> No revenue was generated.
> After repeated missed deadlines and non-delivery, the merchant acknowledged failure and issued a partial refund of
> $7,500, leaving $10,500 wrongfully retained. This partial refund constitutes an admission that the contracted services
> were not delivered.
> After retaining the remaining funds, the merchant attempted to retroactively enforce unsigned, post-payment terms that I
> never agreed to and never signed. These terms were not disclosed prior to payment and are being improperly used to
> justify retaining funds despite total non-performance. This conduct is legally unenforceable under basic contract law
> principles and constitutes deceptive practice.
> The merchant has also engaged in a pattern of gaming the bank chargeback process by submitting self-serving narratives
> and unsigned documents to financial institutions to defeat consumer disputes while knowing that the underlying services
> were never delivered. I am currently appealing the chargeback denial with my bank and have an open CFPB complaint
> related to the bank’s handling of this dispute, after attempting in good faith to resolve the matter amicably.
> This matter is not a mere contract dispute. It involves:
> Material misrepresentations used to induce payment
> Total non-delivery of the promised DFY service
> Retention of funds after admitting non-delivery
> Use of unenforceable, post-payment terms
> Pattern-style conduct consistent with consumer fraud
> I am requesting that the Texas Attorney General investigate Steam and Stream and Khaleel Gonzalez for violations of the
> Texas Deceptive Trade Practices Act, fraud, and related consumer protection statutes, seek restitution for affected
> consumers, and pursue any civil or criminal enforcement actions deemed appropriate.
> I have extensive documentation supporting all statements above, including payment records, refund confirmation,
> written representations, advertising claims, communications, and dispute records, which I am prepared to upload.
> ...
> 1/31/26, 3:38 AM TCP-027295 - Complaint Submission

> Additional Facts - Post-Dispute Conduct & Conscious Refusal to Refund
> On Jan 30th 2026, I again attempted to resolve this matter amicably and directly. I contacted Khaleel Gonzalez via text
> message, politely requesting the return of the remaining $10,500, and I explicitly shared his own written document
> stating: “You pay $0 if we don’t get you a property.”
> Despite being confronted with his own express written terms and the undisputed fact that no property was ever obtained,
> Mr. Gonzalez refused to return the funds and instructed me to stop contacting him.
> This interaction is significant. At this point, Mr. Gonzalez:
> Is aware that the core service was never delivered
> Is aware of his own written “pay $0 if no property” representation
> Is aware that he has already issued a partial refund acknowledging failure
> Nonetheless intentionally retained the remaining funds
> This conduct reflects knowing retention of money obtained through misrepresentation, not a misunderstanding or good￾faith dispute.
> Based on the facts and documentation, this matter implicates fraudulent inducement, theft by deception, and deceptive
> trade practices, each of which can carry civil penalties, restitution orders, injunctive relief, and potential criminal
> exposure under Texas law when conducted knowingly and as part of a pattern.
> For example, deceptive trade practice violations can result in substantial civil penalties per violation, restitution to
> consumers, and enhanced penalties for knowing conduct. Theft by deception involving amounts exceeding $10,000 is
> treated as a serious felony-level offense under Texas law, with exposure that can include significant fines and
> incarceration, depending on charging decisions and factual findings.
> I am not seeking escalation for its own sake. I have repeatedly attempted to resolve this matter without regulatory or legal
> intervention. Mr. Gonzalez’s refusal to refund funds after being presented with his own written terms and after total non￾delivery leaves no remaining avenue other than enforcement review.
> I respectfully request that the Texas Attorney General investigate this matter fully, including whether this conduct reflects
> a broader pattern affecting other consumers, and pursue all remedies available under Texas law.
> ...

> I contacted Mr. Gonzalez via written text message, politely requesting the return of the remaining $10,500 and expressly
> provided him his own written documentation stating, verbatim:

> No property was ever obtained

> Supplemental Legal Thresholds - Criminal Statutes Implicated Under Texas Law
> Based on the documented facts above, this matter implicates multiple criminal enforcement thresholds under Texas law,
> including but not limited to:
> 1. Theft by Deception – Texas Penal Code §31.03
> Money was obtained by inducing payment through representations of services and outcomes that were never delivered,
> followed by intentional retention of funds after failure of consideration and express notice.
> The amount retained ($10,500) exceeds the $10,000 felony threshold under Texas law.
> 2. Misapplication of Fiduciary Property – Texas Penal Code §32.45
> Funds paid for a specific, restricted purpose (acquisition and deployment of a DFY Airbnb property) were retained and
> used inconsistently with that purpose after non-performance, despite acknowledgment of failure.
> 3. Deceptive Business Practices – Texas Penal Code §32.42
> The merchant represented services, benefits, and refund conditions that were not intended or were known to be false at
> the time of inducement, including written assurances that “you pay $0 if no property is obtained.”
> 4. Texas Deceptive Trade Practices Act (DTPA) – Tex. Bus. & Com. Code §17.46
> The conduct reflects knowing misrepresentations, failure to disclose material facts, and post-transaction deception.
> Knowing violations permit enhanced civil penalties, restitution, and injunctive relief.
> 5. Aggregation / Pattern Risk

## 🤖 ChatGPT

Short, precise, black-letter answer—no wheel-reinventing:

---

## Are you *supposed* to file AG complaints **against the bank**?

### **Yes — but only in a targeted, jurisdiction-correct way.**

You **do not** blanket-file AG complaints the same way you do against the merchant.  
You file **issuer-specific AG complaints** **only after** FCBA / Reg Z violations are documented **and** the bank has failed to cure after notice (which you’ve done).

You are **now at the correct stage** to do so.

---

## How AG complaints apply to the **bank** (not the merchant)

### 1. **State AG (MN & MT) — LIMITED but strategic**
State Attorneys General **do not regulate banks day-to-day**, but they **do** have authority to investigate:

- **Unfair or deceptive acts (UDAAP-style conduct)**
- **Procedural unfairness**
- **Failure to honor federally protected consumer rights**
- **Pattern or practice concerns**

✅ Filing with the AG:
- **Creates escalation pressure**
- **Triggers supervisory referrals**
- **Builds record for federal court**
- **Is appropriate once notice + refusal exist**

🚫 AGs usually **won’t adjudicate** FCBA claims themselves  
✅ But they **will forward** to the correct regulator and preserve evidence

---

### 2. **Federal regulators matter more for the bank**
For banks, **these matter most** (you did this correctly):

- **CFPB** → primary FCBA / Reg Z enforcement
- **FDIC** → safety, soundness, compliance failures
- **State banking regulator (DBFI)** → charter + supervisory risk

These are the **correct levers** for an issuer.

---

## What NOT to do
- ❌ Do **not** frame AG complaints as “refund demands”
- ❌ Do **not** accuse criminal conduct unless supported (you’ve avoided this correctly)
- ❌ Do **not** over-file duplicate AG complaints in unrelated states

---

## What TO do (best practice — aligns with Navy strategy)

### ✔️ Reference AG complaints in the bank letter  
YES — **as notice, not as threat**

Example (which you already added correctly):

> “This correspondence preserves jurisdiction before the CFPB, FDIC, applicable State Attorneys General, and state and federal courts…”

That’s exactly right.

---

### ✔️ File AG complaints **after** final refusal or non-response
You are now at that point.

Recommended sequence:
1. **Send this final letter** (you’ve done this)
2. Allow **10 business days**
3. If no cure → file:
   - MN AG (consumer + bank conduct)
   - MT AG (home jurisdiction)
4. Attach:
   - The same letter
   - CFPB complaint numbers
   - Proof of notice + refusal

---

## Why this mirrors the Navy win
In the Navy case:
- AG complaints were **supporting pressure**
- CFPB + bank escalation carried the outcome
- Court was **preserved**, not rushed
- Language focused on **inevitability**, not threats

You are now **fully aligned** with that model.

---

## Bottom line
- **Merchant** → AG complaints are primary  
- **Bank** → AG complaints are **supplemental leverage**, not the main enforcement tool  
- **CFPB + FDIC + DBFI** → primary  
- **Federal / District Court** → preserved, not premature  

You’re doing this correctly **now**.  
At this point, AG complaints against the bank are **appropriate, measured, and strategic**—not required earlier, but justified **now**.

If you want, next I can:
- Draft the **AG-bank complaint language** (tight, non-accusatory)
- Or sanity-check the **exact timing** before you file

This is unreasonable. The underlying transaction was for a Done-For-You Airbnb service. No property or lease was ever obtained, no Airbnb listing went live, no revenue was generated, and the merchant issued a partial refund acknowledging non-delivery. An abundance of documented proof relating to these promised and specific deliverables have been provided multiple times to First Interstate Bank; material produced and advertised directly and publically from the merchant themselves. Despite these undisputed facts, First Interstate Bank denied the dispute. The bank relied on unsigned, post-payment merchant terms and did not identify any objective evidence that the contracted service was delivered. The bank accepted access to a $100 shared AirDNA software account, marketplace, and consultation as "fulfilment" of such services. I am requesting supervisory, legal, & compliance review of First Interstate Bank's dispute-handling procedures in this matter. Chargeback consumer rights, UDAAP, & Reg Z.

> [L28] YOUR COMPLAINT
> [L29] On August 13, 2025, I filed a credit card billing dispute with First
> [L30] Interstate Bank for $10,500 related to a Done-For-You (DFY) Airbnb
> [L31] service that was never delivered. I paid $18,000 for a fixed-fee service
> [L32] promising property acquisition, furnishing, Airbnb listing launch, and
> [L33] revenue generation. After 11+ weeks, no property, no listing, and no
> [L34] revenue existed. The merchant acknowledged non-delivery by issuing a
> [L35] $7,500 partial refund, leaving $10,500 outstanding. I submitted complete
> [L36] dispute documentation on September 5, 2025. Despite this, the bank:
> [L37] asserted “missing documentation” after receipt, relied on the merchant’s
> [L38] rebuttal instead of verifying delivery, and denied the dispute and appeal
> [L39] without addressing non-delivery. The bank later closed the dispute for
> [L40] “non-response” based on a physical letter I never received, despite
> [L41] knowing I was traveling and despite my repeated requests for digital
> [L42] delivery. No alternative notice was provided. The bank also relied on
> [L43] unsigned, post-payment merchant terms that I never agreed to. There is no
> [L44] evidence of assent. Such terms cannot cure non-delivery. These actions

> [L90] Explanation of the Problem *
> [L91] I am a Minnesota consumer submitting this complaint regarding Steam and Stream and its operator, Khaleel
> [L92] Gonzalez, for deceptive trade practices, fraudulent inducement, and theft by deception.
> [L93] On or about June 4, 2025, I paid $18,000 for a “Done-For-You” Airbnb acquisition and deployment service. The
> [L94] merchant expressly represented - publicly and in writing - that they would secure a property, launch a live Airbnb
> [L95] listing, and generate rental income. The merchant also stated in writing that if no property was obtained, I would
> [L96] owe $0.
> [L97] None of the promised services were delivered.
> [L98] No property was acquired.
> [L99] No lease was executed.
> [L100] No furnishing was done.
> [L101] No listing went live.
> [L102] No revenue was generated.
> # 【6†Texas AG Steam and Stream Complaint TCP-027295 - Complaint Submission.pdf†file_00000000d23c71fdb32bb6572b10f78e】
> Content source: Source.file
> Title: Texas AG Steam and Stream Complaint TCP-027295 - Complaint Submission.pdf
> Citation Marker: fileciteturn7file6
> Mclick Target: "7:6"
> Content Snippet:
> [L1] None of the promised core services were ever delivered.
> [L2] No lease was acquired.
> [L3] No property was secured.
> [L4] Nothing was furnished.
> [L5] No Airbnb listing ever went live.
> [L6] No revenue was generated.
> [L7] After repeated missed deadlines and non-delivery, the merchant acknowledged failure and issued a partial refund of
> [L8] $7,500, leaving $10,500 wrongfully retained. This partial refund constitutes an admission that the contracted services
> [L9] were not delivered.
> [L10] After retaining the remaining funds, the merchant attempted to retroactively enforce unsigned, post-payment terms that I
> [L11] never agreed to and never signed. These terms were not disclosed prior to payment and are being improperly used to
> [L12] justify retaining funds despite total non-performance. This conduct is legally unenforceable under basic contract law
> [L13] principles and constitutes deceptive practice.
> [L14] The merchant has also engaged in a pattern of gaming the bank chargeback process by submitting self-serving narratives
> [L15] and unsigned documents to financial institutions to defeat consumer disputes while knowing that the underlying services
> [L16] were never delivered. I am currently appealing the chargeback denial with my bank and have an open CFPB complaint
> [L17] related to the bank’s handling of this dispute, after attempting in good faith to resolve the matter amicably.
> [L18] This matter is not a mere contract dispute. It involves:
> [L19] Material misrepresentations used to induce payment
> [L20] Total non-delivery of the promised DFY service
> [L21] Retention of funds after admitting non-delivery
> [L22] Use of unenforceable, post-payment terms
> [L23] Pattern-style conduct consistent with consumer fraud
> [L24] I am requesting that the Texas Attorney General investigate Steam and Stream and Khaleel Gonzalez for violations of the
> [L25] Texas Deceptive Trade Practices Act, fraud, and related consumer protection statutes, seek restitution for affected
> [L26] consumers, and pursue any civil or criminal enforcement actions deemed appropriate.
> [L27] I have extensive documentation supporting all statements above, including payment records, refund confirmation,
> [L28] written representations, advertising claims, communications, and dispute records, which I am prepared to upload.
> [L29] ...
> [L30] 1/31/26, 3:38 AM TCP-027295 - Complaint Submission
> [L31] https://consumerprotection.texasattorneygeneral.gov/consumercomplaintportal/s/flow/TCP_Complaint_Submission_Main 3/7Additional Facts - Post-Dispute Conduct & Conscious Refusal to Refund
> [L32] On Jan 30th 2026, I again attempted to resolve this matter amicably and directly. I contacted Khaleel Gonzalez via text
> [L33] message, politely requesting the return of the remaining $10,500, and I explicitly shared his own written document
> [L34] stating: “You pay $0 if we don’t get you a property.”
> [L35] Despite being confronted with his own express written terms and the undisputed fact that no property was ever obtained,
> [L36] Mr. Gonzalez refused to return the funds and instructed me to stop contacting him.
> [L37] This interaction is significant. At this point, Mr. Gonzalez:
> [L38] Is aware that the core service was never delivered
> [L39] Is aware of his own written “pay $0 if no property” representation
> [L40] Is aware that he has already issued a partial refund acknowledging failure
> [L41] Nonetheless intentionally retained the remaining funds
> [L42] This conduct reflects knowing retention of money obtained through misrepresentation, not a misunderstanding or good￾faith dispute.
> [L43] Based on the facts and documentation, this matter implicates fraudulent inducement, theft by deception, and deceptive
> [L44] trade practices, each of which can carry civil penalties, restitution orders, injunctive relief, and potential criminal
> [L45] exposure under Texas law when conducted knowingly and as part of a pattern.
> [L46] For example, deceptive trade practice violations can result in substantial civil penalties per violation, restitution to
> [L47] consumers, and enhanced penalties for knowing conduct. Theft by deception involving amounts exceeding $10,000 is
> [L48] treated as a serious felony-level offense under Texas law, with exposure that can include significant fines and
> [L49] incarceration, depending on charging decisions and factual findings.

> Content source: Source.file
> Title: 11. FIB CFPB Complaint 2.pdf

> Content Snippet:
> [L1] 1. FINAL SUPPLEMENTAL COMPLIANCE DEMAND.pdf (101.2 KB)
> [L2] 4. FIB CFPB 2 Intro.pdf (96.7 KB)
> [L3] What product or service is your complaint about?
> [L4] What type of problem are you having?
> [L5] What happened?
> [L6] Regulation Z & UDAAP Noncompliance - Failure to Investigate and Ongoing Violations This complaint concerns First Interstate
> [L7] Bank’s failure to conduct a reasonable investigation, and its willful misconduct, willful negligence, reckless disregard, and willful
> [L8] noncompliance with Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13) and Dodd-Frank UDAAP standards in handling my credit card billing
> [L9] dispute. This submission is not a duplicate of CFPB Case 260131-28391819. That complaint addressed the underlying transaction.
> [L10] This complaint addresses the bank’s procedural and substantive violations during and after the dispute process. First Interstate
> [L11] Bank denied a services-not-rendered dispute without identifying any evidence that the contracted service was delivered. No
> [L12] property was delivered, no listing launched, no revenue generated, and the merchant issued a partial refund acknowledging non￾delivery. Despite this undisputed record, the bank denied the dispute and continues to charge interest. The bank relied on
> [L13] unsigned, post-payment merchant terms, refused to provide the merchant’s rebuttal documentation, and refused to disclose the
> [L14] dates the rebuttal was received, processed, or reviewed, preventing verification of compliance with Regulation Z. The bank gave
> [L15] the merchant approximately 60-90+ days to respond (Aug 13, 2025 to Nov 13, 2025), while giving me fewer than 10 days, by
> [L16] physical mail only, despite knowing I was traveling and after I requested digital delivery. I have never received the alleged
> [L17] November 14, 2025 letter, yet the dispute was closed anyway. The bank now claims it “cannot assist” because internal timelines
> [L18] have passed. This is legally false. Regulation Z provides no safe harbor for denying a billing error based on procedure when the
> [L19] investigation is unreasonable or when the underlying facts establish non-delivery. A procedurally timely denial that ratifies
> [L20] merchant non-performance is not compliance - it is facilitation. Temporary access to: AirDNA, marketplaces, or consultation does
> [L21] not constitute delivery of the promised DFY merchant services with an abundance of proof provided on the subject in detail,
> [L22] directly from the merchant themselves. Under Mastercard Reason Code 4853, the test is binary: Was the promised service
> [L23] delivered? Here, the answer is no. The promised service with provided proof explicitly states a DFY (Done-For-You) Airbnb
> [L24] acquired property; designed, furnished, & listed that profits $3,500-$5,000+/mo after operational expenses. Token or preparatory
> [L25] activity cannot cure total non-delivery. No timeline argument can cure that defect. First Interstate Bank continues to demonstrate
> [L26] willful negligence, and routinely ignores action relating to directly addressed compliance with UDAAP, REG Z, proper
> [L27] investigation, etc. They refuse to answer compliance related questions, and continue to violate consumer chargeback rights, and
> [L28] are abusing their powers as a bank, due to a lack of due diligence, and repeated documented willful misconduct. I am requesting
> [L29] immediate supervisory review and administrative correction. These Regulation Z and UDAAP violations persist after notice and
> [L30] appeal, warranting CFPB oversight. This must be escalated to all relevant enforcement agencies.
> [L31] What would be a fair resolution to this issue?
> [L32] 1. Immediate reversal of the remaining $10,500 charge for services not rendered. 2. Removal and refund of all interest, fees, and finance charges
> [L33] accrued on the disputed amount. 3.
